# sts-for-ai showcase, text edition for language models

This is the full edition: everything in llms.md plus every real-game fight, the full list of everything tried, and each statistic's setting, counting notes and data.

This is the showcase page of sts-for-ai (a home-built Slay the Spire combat engine and an AI that plays it) as one Markdown file. The web page is showcase/index.html in this repository; this file holds the same content with every chart written out as a table and every hover tip written inline. It was generated by `showcase/llm.py` from the hand-written page and its data files (data written 2026-11-14 22:08).

How to read it:

- **Every number belongs to a setting.** The project changed its tests, players and rules many times in 23 days. A number is tagged `[setting: short tag | full setting]`; two numbers with different settings must not be compared or pooled.
- **Ascension 0 and Ascension 20 results are separate.** So are the real-game run settings (run rules 1 to 5, fight guard). Real-game boss counts come in two forms: "of those reached" (runs that got to the Act 1 boss) and "of runs" (all runs in that setting), so 22 of 24 and 22 of 27 describe the same Ascension 0 runs.
- **Players:** "hand-written player" (a formula, the baseline), "net, one turn deep", and "the product" (the 29 October net searching two turns deep with an HP price and line step). From 13 November the product also prices turn-start powers and leftover block.
- **The gauntlet** is the project's own Act 1 test (11 fights, starter deck, no relics beyond Burning Blood, no potions); it is not the real game. "Clears" = share of runs that beat the Act 1 boss. "se" = standard error.
- **Sections:** the story in eight stretches; how the work was done; a verdict on the AI-run research; results and six findings; how the AI plays; the real game on 14 November, run by run; what's next; every thing tried; the statistics tab; glossary.

Slay the Spire · Ironclad · Act 1 · Ascension 0

# An Ironclad that thinks *two turns ahead*

sts-for-ai is a home-built Slay the Spire combat engine and an AI that plays it. One hobbyist started it on 23 October; for most of the 23 days since, AI roles they set up ran the research, with the owner steering from time to time. This page is its history: everything that was tried, how the work was done, and how well that worked. Today it beats the Act 1 boss in about four runs of five on our test, and it is playing the real game.

Share of runs that beat the Act 1 boss

Stretch 1 · **23–24 Oct** 61 commits

### The engine and the first question

**How the work was done.** The owner and Claude worked side by side in a spec-first loop: the owner confirms a spec, Claude builds it step by step against checks a program can run, and every design call goes into a decisions log. That log kept growing for the whole project and holds 499 entries today.

##### Built

- A combat engine in Rust: Ironclad, Act 1, Ascension 0. 24 cards, the hallway monsters, Gremlin Nob, Lagavulin, three Sentries, Slime Boss with its split.

- Two twin nets trained on 30,000 games: one reads cards by what they do, one by their names.

- The Silent as a second character, to test transfer.

##### Learned

- On decks with five held-out cards (Twin Strike, Iron Wave, Thunderclap, Pommel Strike, Uppercut), the "what it does" net predicted fight outcomes with 50% less error. [setting: prediction error · 10-23 | Check 6: value-prediction error on games with held-out cards, effects net vs ids net, both trained on the same 30,000 games; bar 20%. Commit 39de9ebf, 2026-10-23.]

- Nets trained only on Ironclad predicted Silent fights with 27% less error than the names net. [setting: prediction error · Silent · 10-24 | Check 8: Silent tier-A decks, MSE 0.0753 vs 0.1034, bar 20%. Commit 647225dd, 2026-10-24.]

- Valuing an unseen power card missed its bar (18.3% against 20%). Demon Form was the hardest.

**Ruler** How far a net's predicted fight score is from the real one, on cards it never trained on.

**Moment:** Hemokinesis deals 15, not 16, with Rupture in play. The game works out the card's damage before Rupture's Strength lands, and the engine copies that.

**15 things tried in this stretch:** Worked 3, Didn't work 3, Partly 1, Built 7, Dropped 1. (Every one is listed under "Everything tried", stretch 1.)

Stretch 2 · **25–28 Oct** 110 commits

### Self-play, with the owner at the desk

**How the work was done.** Still at the desk. Training ran as overnight sweeps of many recipe variants, some on Google Colab to borrow extra machines, and the days went to reading what the night had produced.

##### Built

- A self-play loop with overnight sweeps of many training recipes.

- The net rewritten in Rust so search can call it fast.

- An exact solver that finds provably best play near the end of a fight, and a puzzle bench from it.

- A design for a general rules engine, so new mechanics can come in as data.

##### Learned

- Ten rounds of self-play stayed below the hand-written player: 0.475 / 0.445 wins against its 0.528 / 0.496 (training cards / held-out cards). [setting: tight fights v1 · 10-26 | Tight fights v1, 2 seeds, 1,079 games per group per round, 10 rounds. learned-play-results.md 'Amendment 3 sweep and why rounds don't improve', 2026-10-26.]

- The curriculum night reached the hand-written player, not past it: 0.50–0.52 against 0.512. [setting: tight fights v1 · 10-28 | Tight fights v1, 2 seeds, 10 rounds; the pass bar needed a lead of about 0.085, the best round led by 0.035. 'Curriculum night (2026-10-28)'.]

**Ruler** Tight fights: fights started at the HP where the hand-written player wins about half the time, so small differences in play show.

**Moment:** One position's draw outcomes kept the exact solver busy for hours in the middle of the night.

**14 things tried in this stretch:** Worked 2, Didn't work 3, Partly 1, Built 8. (Every one is listed under "Everything tried", stretch 2.)

Stretch 3 · **29–31 Oct** 219 commits

### Claude runs the lab day to day

**How the work was done.** On the evening of 29 October the owner stepped away, as planned, and handed the day-to-day to one Claude session acting as manager, keeping its state in a manager file and deciding on the owner's behalf. On the 30th it got a job queue so experiments ran unattended. The same day, looking for faster progress, the owner asked for the first big-picture review: several analyst agents read all the data and skeptic agents checked their claims.

##### Built

- From the evening of 29 October the owner was away, and a Claude session they set up ran experiments from a job queue around the clock.

- Hexaghost and The Guardian (Mode Shift, Sharp Hide, Burn), so all three Act 1 bosses were in.

- A new close-fights test (1,226 fights), a champion ladder for promoting nets, and daily big-picture reviews by several analyst agents.

- Two-turn search, fixed twice before it worked.

##### Learned

- The night's "passes" were luck. Retrained with three seeds, their held-out leads were +0.006, +0.003, −0.004, −0.003. [setting: tight2 · 3 seeds · 10-29 | Seed check: the four 10-28 rounds retrained at three seeds, tight2 train and held-out halves. 'Seed check: do the night's gate passes hold up?', 2026-10-29.]

- The first champion, the 29 October net, beat the hand-written player by +0.037 over 1,150 paired fights, and by +0.132 on boss fights. It is still the product's net. [setting: ladder · 1 turn · 10-30 | Champion ladder test 0001: fresh stratified fights, one turn deep; train +0.064, held-out +0.011; boss +0.132, elite +0.068, hallway +0.020. 2026-10-30.]

- Two turns beat one once fixed: +0.056 wins (se 0.014) on 1,328 elite and boss fights. [setting: tight2 elites+bosses · 10-31 | Node cap 400,000, heuristic leaves, 4 sampled draws; two-turn minus one-turn win rate on train elite+boss fights of tight2; held-out +0.016 (0.015). depth-pilot-2026-10-30.md.]

**Ruler** tight2 (1,226 close fights) for training recipes; the champion ladder for promoting a net.

**Moment:** The nets refused to play Demon Form. In 156 held-out boss fights the hand-written player played it at 41% of first chances, the nets at 6.5%. Against Hexaghost that meant 0.54 wins for the hand-written player and 0.07 to 0.11 for the nets.

**46 things tried in this stretch:** Worked 12, Didn't work 19, Partly 4, Built 11. (Every one is listed under "Everything tried", stretch 3.)

Stretch 4 · **1–2 Nov** 236 commits

### From single fights to an Act

**How the work was done.** The manager ran read after read against the new gauntlet. On 2 November the owner was back at the desk for a day: an approach review by 99 read-only agents that they had asked for, the rules-engine series they asked for, built overnight on a branch, a training rule (train only when there is something new to learn), and the groundwork for the next way of working: a lanes runner, a fact store and a product registry.

##### Built

- An HP price in the search, with no retraining.

- The 11-fight gauntlet, then card rewards picked by trial fights, then trial fights against the boss too.

- Probes for the campfire, card removal and skipping elites.

- Overnight, on a branch: Body Slam, Heavy Blade, Perfected Strike and Whirlwind's X cost, picks inside a card (Survivor, Burst, Hologram), monster move patterns as data.

##### Learned

- The nets lost about twice the hand-written player's HP per fight; the HP price saved 2.75 HP per fight. [setting: run-HP fights · 11-01 | Run-HP fights (fights at run-like start HP), champion with and without @hpb0.01. Decisions 'HP bonus promoted', 2026-11-01.]

- From the starter deck: skipping every reward clears 0.000, random picks 0.364, trial-fight picks 0.548. [setting: starter · 1 turn · 500 runs · 11-02 | Starter deck, 10-29 net @hpb0.01 one turn deep, 500 sequences, before sim-boss. 'Card rewards: deck choice is a goal-2 lever as big as depth', 2026-11-02.]

- Looking at the boss when picking: 0.695 against 0.540. [setting: sim-boss · 1 turn · 162 runs · 11-02 | Sim-boss vs plain sim, 10-29 net @hpb0.01 one turn deep, protocol stop at 162 of 200 sequences; boss deaths 44 vs 69. 2026-11-02.]

- The first headline: 0.804 clears two turns deep, +0.124 over one turn. [setting: headline · 2 turns · runs 0–499 · 11-02 | Headline setting, 10-29 net @hpe0.01 two turns deep (plain two-turn, before line step), runs 0–499; +0.124 (0.022) over one turn (0.680), +0.270 over the heuristic (0.534). 2026-11-02.]

**Ruler** The gauntlet: clears over 500 runs, paired run by run. First on fixed decks, from 2 November on the headline setting (starter deck, trial-fight picks with the boss in view).

**Moment:** Gremlin Nob against the HP price. Pricing HP made the net block more, and every Skill feeds Nob Strength. Skills per Nob fight: hand-written 1.32, net 1.68, net with the price 2.28, with a heavy price 4.13. Wins fell from 0.994 to 0.833. And with the boss in view, the card picker started drafting scaling: Spot Weakness 86 times where it had taken it almost never.

**74 things tried in this stretch:** Worked 21, Didn't work 12, Partly 8, Built 31, Dropped 2. (Every one is listed under "Everything tried", stretch 4.)

Stretch 5 · **3–5 Nov** 340 commits

### The AI roles take over

**How the work was done.** On 3 November the single manager became scheduled roles: a steward that keeps the machine healthy and writes a daily digest, a navigator that ranks ideas and opens lanes, and lane workers that each run one lane, a short project with a question, a CPU-hour cap and an end date. The first four lanes opened that morning. On the 5th the owner chose one turn deep for every comparison, because a two-turn game costs about 100 one-turn games.

##### Built

- Three scheduled AI roles: a steward, a navigator and lane workers (see "Who ran it").

- Line step: the same two-turn decisions at 70% of the time.

- A goal-1 test: official monsters with changes like Sharp Hide 2, Ritual 2 or more HP.

- The owner's design call: Act 1 goes in as content files, not code, so new content needs no engine change.

##### Learned

- The headline as quoted today: 0.834 on runs 0–499, 0.780 on held-back runs 500–999. [setting: headline · 2 turns · 11-03/05 | Product (10-29 net @hpe0.01, two turns, line step), headline setting. Runs 0–499 read 2026-11-03; 500–999 read 2026-11-05 (se 0.019); both books 0.807.]

- Training on the two-turn player's games lost HP: −1.37 and −1.02 on two seeds. [setting: cell gauntlet · 1 turn · n 150 · 11-03 | Two-turn self-play round: 10-29 net fine-tuned on 3,000 two-turn product games, one turn deep @hpb0.01 on the cell gauntlet, n 150 (se 0.83 / 0.70). 2026-11-03.]

- Changes kept their sign two turns deep at about a third of their size, and a two-turn game costs about 100 one-turn games. From 5 November every comparison plays one turn deep.

**Ruler** The headline two turns deep, then (from 5 November) one turn deep against the one-turn control at 0.680.

**Moment:** Skipping elites kept winning, because the engine had no relics for an elite to drop.

**35 things tried in this stretch:** Worked 10, Didn't work 6, Partly 7, Built 11, Dropped 1. (Every one is listed under "Everything tried", stretch 5.)

Stretch 6 · **6–9 Nov** 763 commits

### Real Act 1 goes in

**How the work was done.** The busiest stretch: 763 commits and up to 8 lanes opened in a day. On 9 November the owner started working on Act 1 content directly in their own sessions, since the lane process was slow for that kind of work.

##### Built

- The seven missing monsters as data: Blue and Red Slaver, Fat, Sneaky, Mad and Shield Gremlins, Gremlin Wizard.

- Ironclad cards block by block: 31 of the 40 missing by 9 November.

- A smaller game state (1,292 to 744 bytes): search 17.6% faster, identical games.

- A test of 24 made-up monster mechanics on official elites.

- From 9 November the owner worked on Act 1 content directly, alongside the lanes.

##### Learned

- Of ten real cards the nets never saw, only Demon Form cost them: −4.26 HP on decks with it, +5.28 without. [setting: headline · 1 turn · held-out cards · 11-06 | Product recipe (three seeds) minus heuristic, one turn deep, held-out card half of both headline books. Decisions 'After yardstick-cards step 1', 2026-11-06.]

- Real cards in the offers: 0.834 to 0.684 for the product, and the order of players holds. [setting: pack offers · 500 runs · 11-07 | 27 act1-ironclad pack cards in both halves' offers, each player vs its own control, headline, n 500. Read 2026-11-07.]

- Made-up mechanics cost the net 1.28 HP per elite fight on average; "deals damage when attacked" on Gremlin Nob cost 12.80. [setting: generated elites · 1 turn · 11-07 | Generated-enemies bench, 24 mechanics, 675 elite starts x 16, one turn deep @hpb0.01@tsl1, net minus heuristic against a stat-matched control; mean over 21 mechanics. 2026-11-07.]

**Ruler** One turn deep against the one-turn control; goal 1 in HP per elite fight against the hand-written player.

**Moment:** The real Exordium Thugs are meaner. They cost 3.28 HP more than the engine's old stand-in, because the real encounter can hold a Blue or Red Slaver.

**65 things tried in this stretch:** Worked 24, Didn't work 12, Partly 11, Built 17, Dropped 1. (Every one is listed under "Everything tried", stretch 6.)

Stretch 7 · **10–12 Nov** 431 commits

### New nets for new content; relics and potions

**How the work was done.** Lanes kept opening and closing within a day, mostly on unseen content. The big-picture reviews started to say that the product had not moved since 3 November. Beside the lanes the owner built relics and potions in their own sessions and set the plan for the last two days.

##### Built

- Nets trained from scratch on games with the new Act 1 cards, then with 18 made-up monster mechanics, then with a grid of 100 made-up mechanics.

- Relics as their own thing in the engine, with hooks and counters: 0 to 52 by the night of 12 November. Potions: 0 to 25.

- The game's own code became the authority for every value.

##### Learned

- From-scratch nets gained on the new-card test, +3.33 HP one turn deep, and the gain carried whole to two turns: +4.57 HP, +0.097 clears. [setting: pack offers · 3 seeds · 11-10/11 | Three seeds, 31-card pack in the offers. One turn: n 500, runs 0–499, se 0.83. Two turns (product flags) vs the product: runs 0–249, +4.57 (1.19) HP, +0.097 (0.030) clears. 2026-11-10/11.]

- On official content their gain was Demon Form again: +17.7 to +20.2 HP where both players took it, −2.14 HP on the 345 runs where nobody did. [setting: headline · 1 turn · 11-10 | Three pack nets one turn deep on the plain headline: +0.78 (0.89) HP overall, a fail against the +1.5 HP bar. 2026-11-10.]

- Training on made-up mechanics removed 0.76 of the cost of mechanics never seen. [setting: generated elites · 3 seeds · 11-11 | Generated-pack nets, 3 seeds, 675 official elite starts x 8, one turn deep; 6 held-out mechanics +5.33 → +1.28 HP per elite fight. 2026-11-11.]

**Ruler** One turn deep; content gains on the pack-offers headline against its own control; goal 1 as the share of an unseen mechanic's cost a net removes.

**Moment:** The relic count climbed by the hour: 23 on 10 November, 40 at lunchtime on the 12th, 52 by 11 pm.

**43 things tried in this stretch:** Worked 13, Didn't work 11, Partly 5, Built 13, Still open 1. (Every one is listed under "Everything tried", stretch 7.)

Stretch 8 · **13–14 Nov** 416 commits so far

### The product changes; the real game

**How the work was done.** The owner changed the pass rule to 'level and more general is a pass', and installed the game. On the 14th Claude sessions played the real game through the bridge and fixed what it found, fixed Act 1 engine rules, and wrote this page.

##### Built

- The owner added a pass rule: a change that is more correct or more general passes when it reads level, without a gain.

- The first product change since 3 November: turn-start powers and leftover block priced, with a matching card picker.

- The game installed; its code read straight from the jar (Paper Phrog lives inside Vulnerable: ×1.75 instead of ×1.5 on monsters).

- The bridge to the real game, tested offline on the 13th; whole runs from the morning of the 14th.

- Engine work on the 14th: Ascension 20 for every Act 1 monster, built from the game's code; the last four Ironclad cards, so all 75 load; and a new rules version (f59b662a) whose recorded test games cover the fixes the live runs found.

##### Learned

- Pricing turn-start powers one turn deep: +0.56 HP, 0.696 clears, and Demon Form back to 0.19 of offers. [setting: headline · 1 turn · 500 runs · 11-13 | 10-29 net @hpb0.01@tsl1, plain trial fights, headline runs 0–499, n 500, against the one-turn control (0.680). 2026-11-13.]

- The product with both prices: 0.832 against 0.834, level, adopted. [setting: headline · 2 turns · 500 runs · 11-13 | Product @hpe0.01@tsl1@lcb1 with chooser @hpe0.01@lcb1, two turns deep, headline runs 0–499, end HP +0.08 (0.51). Job 0986, 2026-11-13. New controls: 0.832 two turns, 0.716 one turn.]

**Ruler** The headline again, re-based on 13 November: 0.832 two turns, 0.716 one turn [setting: one-turn control · 1 turn · 500 runs · 11-13 | One-turn control: the 10-29 net @hpb0.01@tsl1@lcb1 one turn deep, card picks by trial fights @hpb0.01@lcb1, headline setting, runs 0–499, 0.716 clears. File results/gauntlet/net1029-hpb0.01-tsl1-lcb1-starter-simboss.json. Two-turn control: the product with both prices, 0.832 (job 0986), 2026-11-13.]. The real game is outside every ruler.

**Moment:** The first product change in ten days was a tie, on purpose. It prices things more correctly, which matters once relics that act at turn start come in.

**26 things tried in this stretch:** Worked 5, Didn't work 1, Built 18, Still open 2. (Every one is listed under "Everything tried", stretch 8.)

Behind the scenes

## How the work was done

A one-person hobby project, set up so the research could keep going while its owner was away. The way of working changed almost as often as the tests did. Here is how it grew, step by step.

1. 23 Oct

**Spec first.** The owner writes what they want, Claude writes a spec, the owner confirms it, and Claude builds it in steps that each end in a check a program can run. Every design call goes into a decisions log.

2. 25 Oct

**Overnight sweeps.** Training recipes run in batches at night, some on Google Colab; the morning is for reading results.

3. 29 Oct

**A manager for the time away.** One Claude session keeps a manager file with its state and decides on the owner's behalf, with standing instructions from the owner: decide, record why and the alternatives, prefer what can be undone, and keep work moving without waiting on the owner.

4. 30 Oct

**A job queue and big-picture reviews.** Experiments go into a queue that runs them unattended. After the owner asked for faster progress and more data analysis, a saved review workflow was set up to run about once a day and after any major result: several analyst agents read all the data through different lenses, a synthesis follows, and skeptic agents check the key claims. 13 such reviews ran.

5. 2 Nov

**A fact store, a product registry, a training rule.** Facts go into a store of sealed claims that cite their source files and turn "suspect" when a source changes; the best player lives in one registry file; training runs only when the net has something new to learn.

6. 3 Nov

**Roles and lanes.** The manager is split into scheduled roles (below). Research runs in lanes: short projects with a question, a predicted chance of success, a CPU-hour cap, an end date and a written "reopen if". A dashboard shows the machine, the queue and every session.

7. 5 Nov

**One turn deep for comparisons.** The owner's choice: every change is judged one turn deep, where a read costs minutes; the two-turn product is the reported score, not the ruler.

9. 7 Nov

**Reads without a session.** A family of queued jobs can run from one session to its verdict, so a lane needs no worker checking in between jobs.

10. 9 Nov

**The owner works on Act 1 content directly.** A lane spent about 3.5 hours of spec, review and speed checks on two cards, so the owner moved Act 1 content into their own sessions.

11. 13 Nov

**Curator, "level is a pass".** A daily curator role keeps the fact store current. A change that is more correct or more general passes when it reads level, without a gain.

Haiku

#### Steward

Checks the machine's health first and fixes what is red. Writes the daily digest. Never plans or picks research.

Opus

#### Navigator

Reads results, writes big-picture reviews, ranks the backlog and opens lanes. Never edits code.

Opus · up to two at once

#### Lane workers

Each works one lane: writes its spec and code, queues its runs, reads the results and closes the lane with a verdict.

Sonnet

#### Curator

Keeps the fact store current: re-checks facts that went stale, merges duplicates, shortens long ones.

**1,091** queued jobs finished; every result names its job

**499** decision entries; 80 are the owner's own (55 of them signed after leaving), 338 were made on the owner's behalf, the rest are lane records and build notes

**426** active facts on 14 Nov, each citing its files (636 stored, 210 retired); a rules change on 13 Nov flagged 31 stale, and the real-game day left 48 suspect

**58** lanes opened from 3 to 13 November; 55 closed with a written verdict

**13** big-picture reviews, from 30 October on

Did the AI-run research work?

## Fast and honest at trying things; slow to move the headline

Every lane wrote down, before it ran, how likely it thought success was and how much CPU it would need. Those records make it possible to grade the setup.

**55** lanes closed in 11 days

**32** of them closed the day they opened

**25** worked or built what they set out to (23 + 2)

**21** didn't work, each with a "reopen if"

**~30%** of the machine's thread-hours used by queued work

Yes. None of the ten long shots worked, about half of the even bets did, and most of the likely ones did.

No. The rules said machine time was the bottleneck, but queued work filled about 30% of the dev machine's threads. The rest went idle while lanes wrote specs and code, waited for review, or waited for memory.

#### What worked

- **Speed of trying.** 55 lanes closed in 11 days, most within a day, each with a question, a cap, a prediction and a written verdict.

- **Honest records.** Failures were written up as carefully as wins, with what would justify reopening them. This page's "everything tried" list exists because of that.

- **It caught its own mistakes.** The night of 28 October's passes were shown to be seed luck the next day; a boss cost on the mutated-monster test turned out to rest on one lucky control and was withdrawn by a read five times larger.

- **Goal 1 moved.** The owner weighted unseen content at 0.8 of development. On it the lanes went from "new mechanics cost the net" to nets that remove about three quarters of the cost of mechanics they never saw.

- **Engine work landed fast:** content as data files, a smaller game state, all 25 Act 1 monsters and all 75 Ironclad cards by 14 November.

#### What didn't

- **The headline didn't move.** The product stayed the same from 3 to 13 November; about 865 of 1,550 booked thread-hours ran after 3 November without a change to it. Every gain on the Act 1 test came from search and card picks, and most gains found one turn deep shrank to about a third of their size two turns deep.

- **The machine sat idle.** About 70% of its thread-hours went unused, while the rules called machine time the bottleneck.

- **Tests reversed each other.** The recipe that ranked first on one test ranked fourth on the gauntlet; each ruler left out a part of what matters, so rulers kept changing.

- **Coordination cost.** A lane needed hours of process for two cards, so the owner did that work in their own sessions.

- **Facts go stale.** One rules change on 13 November flagged 31 stored facts for re-checking.

Results in brief

## Where it stands, and six things it found

The headline number comes from one fixed test, the gauntlet: Act 1 boiled down to its fights. Here is the test, where the AI's runs end on it, and the findings players argue about, each with the measurement behind it.

- **Starter deck, 80 HP.** Five Strikes, four Defends, Bash. Burning Blood heals 6 after every won fight. No other relics, no potions.

- **Eleven fights in a row.** Three easy hallways, two hard ones, an elite, two hard, an elite, one hard, then a campfire that heals 30% (24 HP), then the boss.

- **Three card offers after each win.** The AI tries each card (and skipping) in 8 short trial fights against the next floor's monsters and 8 against the boss, then takes the best.

- **One loss ends the run.** Every player plays the same seeded runs, so two players can be compared run by run.

- **Not in it:** events, shops, gold, the map, relics beyond Burning Blood, potions. Half the runs offer cards the AI's net never trained on.

**Easy:** Cultist, Jaw Worm, two Louses, small slimes. **Hard:** Looter, Large Slime, Lots of Slimes, three Louses, two Fungi Beasts, Exordium Thugs, Exordium Wildlife. **Elites:** Gremlin Nob, Lagavulin, three Sentries. **Bosses:** Slime Boss, The Guardian, Hexaghost.

**Cards offered (31):** Anger, Bloodletting, Body Slam, Brutality, Carnage, Clash, Cleave, Clothesline, Combust, Demon Form, Dropkick, Flex, FTL, Go for the Eyes, Heavy Blade, Hemokinesis, Inflame, Iron Wave, Metallicize, Perfected Strike, Pommel Strike, Pummel, Rage, Rupture, Shrug It Off, Spot Weakness, Sword Boomerang, Thunderclap, Twin Strike, Uppercut, Wild Strike.

**Why every number here wears a tag.** In 23 days the project changed its rules, its tests and the players it compared against many times. A number only means something next to the test it came from. So every number on this page sits beside a tag like [setting: headline · 2 turns · runs 0–499 | the full setting: which test, which player, how deep it searched, which runs and when], and numbers with different tags are never drawn on one axis.

### Six findings

FINDING 1

### HP at the boss is worth most where that boss's cliff is

We replayed 474 real boss fights from the headline runs, changing only the HP the player walked in with. The result is cause, not correlation: it explains 87% of the slope you see in normal runs.

**Slime Boss** is almost safe from 50 HP (0.92). **The Guardian** is a cliff: 0.07 at 30 HP, 0.49 at 50, 0.88 at 70. **Hexaghost** climbs slowly and is still only 0.90 at 80.

Boss replays play the net one turn deep, 4 seeds at each of six HP levels, 11,376 fights. The death counts come from the product two turns deep over 1,000 runs.

FINDING 2

### An elite costs more than it pays, when it pays no relic

The two elites take 17.3 and 13.8 HP per won fight. Hard hallway fights take 0.5 to 3.6, easy ones about nothing. That HP is what the boss is missing.

Swap every elite for a hard hallway fight and the run ends with 14.0 more HP, and clears go from 0.720 to 0.920. Even with an extra card offer after each elite and a mid-act heal, skipping still clears more (+0.054, about 4 standard errors).

In this test an elite pays one card offer and no relic. That makes skipping the ceiling of a path choice here, not advice for real runs. The real-game controller paths "monsters first, elites last" for the same reason.

FINDING 3

### At the last campfire, just rest

The test's one campfire sits right before the boss. A careful chooser played 64 boss fights for resting and for each possible upgrade, and upgraded only when it was clearly better. It upgraded in 2 runs of 50 and gained nothing: −0.10 HP, the same 0.720 clears.

The heal is 24 HP at 80 max HP, right before the boss, and the boss curve above says those 24 HP are worth a lot. A campfire earlier in the act, with more fights left to use an upgrade, could come out differently.

FINDING 4

### The run you draw decides more than the player

Each square is one of the 500 runs. 200 of them are cleared by every player, even the hand-written one. 36 are lost by all three. The players only differ on the rest.

Between two good players the effect is stronger. The product before and after it began pricing turn-start powers and leftover block ends the same way on 465 of 500 runs; the comparison rides on 35 runs, split 18 to 17.

This is why every comparison here pairs players run by run on the same seeds, and why small changes need hundreds of runs before they show.

FINDING 5

### Thinking deeper beat training harder

Same net, same 500 runs: one turn deep it clears 0.680, two turns deep 0.834 (+0.154, se 0.023). Most of the saved runs are at the second elite and the boss.

Training did not keep up. Self-play stopped improving after one round. 80,000 training games did no better than 40,000. A round trained on the two-turn player's own games played 1.0 to 1.4 HP worse. Every gain on the Act 1 test since 29 October came from how the AI searches and picks cards: the HP price, two turns, trial-fight card picks, looking at the boss when picking.

FINDING 6

### New numbers are free. New mechanics cost.

The project's first goal is an AI that copes with content it has never seen, like a mod. To measure that, official elites were given changes and the net was compared with the hand-written player on them. Bigger numbers cost the net almost nothing extra. A mechanic it never trained against does: Sharp Hide 2 (you take damage for every Attack you play) costs it 6.3 HP per elite fight more than it costs the hand-written player.

**Demon Form, the villain of the project.** Of ten real cards the nets never trained on, only Demon Form cost them: −4.26 HP against the hand-written player on decks that held it. Its Strength arrives at the start of next turn, just past where a one-turn search looks. Scoring the position after turn-start effects fixed it with no training at all, and is now part of the product.

How it plays

## It never picks a card. It picks where to end up.

The engine lists everything you could do, works out exactly where each choice leads, and a scoring function picks the best place to be.

1.

#### Options

Every legal play: each card on each target, each potion, End turn. Copies merge, so three Strikes against two monsters are two options, not six.

2.

#### Where each one leads

The engine plays each option on a copy of the fight. A power the AI has never heard of still shows its effect in the result: more block, more damage, less monster HP.

3.

#### Chance is out in the open

Draws and monster move rolls are chance points. The draw pile is a bag with no hidden order, so the AI can't peek. It lists every outcome with its odds, or samples a few.

4.

#### One turn deep

After End turn the monsters' shown attacks land, and the search stops just before the next draw. That paused position gets a score.

5.

#### Two turns deep

The product doesn't stop there. It samples 4 possible next hands and monster rolls and searches the next turn too, up to 400,000 positions per decision.

6.

#### The score

A small net (about 190,000 weights) guesses how the fight will end: a loss is 0, a win is 2 minus HP lost / 100, and each HP kept earns a little extra. It reads every card as what it does, not its name, so a new card built from familiar parts already means something.

7.

#### Commit to the line

The product plays the whole line its first search found, up to the next draw or random event, and only then searches again. Same decisions in 526 of 526 test decisions, at 70% of the search time.

8.

#### Picking cards

No draft table. For each offer it plays 8 trial fights against the next floor and 8 against the boss with each option in the deck, and keeps the deck that scored best.

The net learned from games it played itself: first from searches scored by a hand-written formula, then from searches with its own previous version. It is the net trained on 29 October, and it is still the product's net today.

The real game · 14 November

## Sitting down at the real Spire

The engine is a copy of the game. Today the AI plays the game itself, on the dev machine, through a mod.

End of 14 November · Ascension 20

At Ascension 20, with the last run rules and the fight guard, it beat the Act 1 boss in 18 of 33 runs.

That is 55%, about 9 points either way [setting: A20 · run rules 5 · fight guard 1 · 33 runs | Ascension 20, run rules 5 (rules 4, and elites last again), fight guard 1 (a winning move the search passed is taken), runs 58-90, the pack net s201 two turns deep. Write-up c54a8fff.]. By boss: Slime Boss 10 of 11, The Guardian 7 of 8, Hexaghost 1 of 9. Hexaghost is the wall: its turn-9 Inferno lands past the two-turn search, and the net has never trained on A20 numbers. 3,777 of 3,782 chance-free steps matched the game (steps with chance aren't compared). At Ascension 0 the same player beat the Act 1 boss in 22 of 27 runs, which is why training moves to A20 next (below, under What's next). The day's settings changed between runs, so each keeps its own table below and none are pooled.

#### Read the screen

CommunicationMod hands a program the game's state. The controller turns it into an engine fight: HP, energy, powers, relics, every pile, each monster's intent and move history. The draw pile is sorted first, because a player can't see its order.

#### Think and play

The product's search, two turns deep, with the net trained from scratch on games that include the real Act 1 cards, since real rewards offer them. Each move goes back as a game command, and the controller checks the game's next state against what the engine predicted. It compares only when every one of the engine's samples agrees, so the match rates cover chance-free steps. A step with chance in it (a draw, a random target, a rolled monster move, so most monster turns) isn't compared: in run 83 the game showed a Mad Gremlin at 21 HP with 7 block where the engine had 17 HP and 11 block, and that fight still counted 202 of 202. Checking those steps against what the game showed is backlog 86.

#### Everything outside fights

Since 06:02 the controller plays whole runs: Neow, the map (monsters first, elites last), rewards, card picks by trial fights, campfires (rest under 60% HP, 75% before the boss), and walks past shops. A fight with an unknown monster falls back to a simple rule. From run 22 on, a run stops when Act 2 starts, on purpose: the engine doesn't model Act 2, so the loop relaunches the game for the next run. Those endings are not deaths. The run layer's rules changed during the day (versions 2 to 5, from run 35), and from run 58 the fight guard takes a winning move the search passed; each setting keeps its own table below.

Why that net: on the test with the real Act 1 cards in the offers, it cleared 0.752 two turns deep against the product's 0.636 on the same runs. [setting: pack offers · 2 turns · runs 0–249 · 11-10 | Pack net seed 201 (runs/2026-11-10-packnet40k-s201-r1-91c8ae0, @hpe0.01, two turns deep, line step) vs the product on the headline setting with the 31-card Act 1 pack in the offers, runs 0–249 (jobs 0820 vs 0777). Read 2026-11-10. The real-game controller runs it as launched: real_game.py launch .../model.pt@hpe0.01 --runs 4.]

### Ascension 0, run rules 1: fights before rest sites at any HP

Per-run data from the bridge's records (read 22:08 on 14 November). Each setting (ascension, run rules, fight guard) is its own table and is never pooled with another. A fight "left to the fallback rule" was played by a simple rule (first playable card on the first living monster), not by the AI. Floors above 17 are Act 2, which the engine does not model; from run 22 on a run stops when Act 2 starts, on purpose, and those endings are not deaths.

| Summary | Value |
|---|---|
| Runs played | 25 (25 finished, 5 stopped at Act 2 on purpose; 2 more left early by a restart) |
| Act 1 bosses beaten, of those reached | 22 of 24 |
| By boss (beaten of reached) | Hexaghost 3/3; Slime Boss 7/7; The Guardian 12/14 |
| Recorded fights won | 314 / 332 |
| Fights the engine played to the end | 267 |
| Chance-free steps matched the game | 3291 / 3321 (99.1%; steps with chance aren't compared) |

#### Run 1 · started 06:05

Act 1 boss beaten · Died on floor 22 in Act 2. Highest floor 22; recorded fights won 13 / 13; chance-free steps matched 89 / 92; fights left to the fallback rule 3.

The first whole run the controller played by itself. The map rule kept it on monster paths, so Act 1 had no elite, and it reached The Guardian with 71 HP. That fight took 15 turns and long thinking, up to 42 seconds a move. While the Guardian attacked, the AI hit back hard: Shockwave on turn 1 for Weak and Vulnerable, then Bash+ and Strikes, and it broke Mode Shift three times. In defensive mode, where Sharp Hide costs 3 HP per Attack, it mostly blocked and used True Grit+ to exhaust Strikes before the next opening. It won at 44 HP (50 after Burning Blood). Act 2 is beyond the engine, so the simple fallback rule fought on and died on floor 22 to a Centurion and a Healer. Relics by then: Ornamental Fan and Runic Pyramid.

#### Run 2 · started 06:28

Act 1 boss beaten · Died on floor 27 in Act 2. Highest floor 27; recorded fights won 17 / 18; chance-free steps matched 164 / 167; fights left to the fallback rule 7.

Neow gave 8 max HP. This time the map led through two elites: three Sentries on floor 6 (88 to 55 HP, all 16 chance-free steps matched the game) and Lagavulin on floor 8 (56 to 52). By floor 11 it was back at 78 HP. The Guardian fell to a Demon Form build: on turn 1 Armaments upgraded Demon Form and it went down, then Disarm and Metallicize. By turn 8 it had 21 Strength, single Strikes and Iron Waves did the rest, and the Guardian's 240 HP were gone on turn 10. It walked in with 83 HP and ended on 61 (67 after Burning Blood). One decision on floor 11 took 111 seconds, the slowest of the day, slowed by a build running beside the game. The deck picked up Demon Form, Metallicize and two Armaments. Bottled Flame and Tiny Chest are relics the engine doesn't load, so they were left out of its picture and recorded. In Act 2 the fallback rule beat a Shelled Parasite, a Spheric Guardian, Byrds with a Chosen and a Snecko, and got to floor 27 on 20 HP, where a Centurion and a Healer ended it (score 256).

#### Run 4 · started 06:49

Act 1 boss beaten · Died on floor 23 in Act 2. Highest floor 23; recorded fights won 15 / 16; chance-free steps matched 139 / 139; fights left to the fallback rule 5.

Heavier going. A Gremlin Gang on floor 5 took 15 HP, and the map led through three elites: Sentries, Lagavulin, Sentries again. By floor 14 it was down to 36 HP. After the campfire it met Slime Boss with 70 HP and lost 1 HP in the whole fight. A slime splits into two that start with the HP it had when it split, and the AI played to that: it took the Spike Slime (L) down to 1 HP before it split, so the two halves had 1 HP each, and split the Acid Slime (L) low too (3 HP each). It blocked the turns the slimes attacked. All 139 chance-free steps matched the game. Lagavulin went to the fallback rule after 5 engine moves, because Snecko Oil had rolled card costs the engine's picture didn't share (fixed since). Relics on the way: Golden Idol, Centennial Puzzle, Gremlin Horn, Du-Vu Doll, Eternal Feather. In Act 2 the fallback rule beat a Chosen, a Spheric Guardian and a Snake Plant, and died on floor 23 to a Sentry and a Spheric Guardian (score 217).

#### Run 6 · started 07:22

Act 1 boss beaten · Died on floor 21 in Act 2. Highest floor 21; recorded fights won 13 / 14; chance-free steps matched 106 / 106; fights left to the fallback rule 3.

The fastest of the morning's runs that got past Act 1: six minutes from Neow to the death screen. The path held no elite, and all 106 chance-free steps matched the game. The Guardian took it from 80 to 43 HP (49 after Burning Blood). The deck by then had Perfected Strike+, Flame Barrier+, Double Tap, two Twin Strikes and Evolve. Its boss relic was Runic Dome, which hides every enemy intent. Without the intents the controller can't rebuild any fight, so even three Cultists on floor 21, monsters the engine knows, went to the fallback rule, and they killed it. The run layer now skips Runic Dome.

#### Run 16 · started 09:07

Act 1 boss lost · Died on floor 16 in Act 1. Highest floor 16; recorded fights won 9 / 10; chance-free steps matched 90 / 91; fights left to the fallback rule 1.

The second boss loss, and a controller bug's fault. The Guardian's Sharp Hide hit the AI for 3 just as it played Blood for Blood+. In the game that card is in limbo while the damage lands, so its cost doesn't drop; the engine thought the card was 1 cheaper, and its planned play was refused. The fallback rule took over on turn 4 at 75 HP and lost a fight the engine had well in hand. The controller now reads Blood for Blood's cost from the game, and the engine's rule for a card in limbo is queued for a fix.

#### Run 17 · started 09:13

The day's first Hexaghost, after Lagavulin had taken it from 76 to 16 HP on floor 6. It met the Hexaghost with 52. ### What the live runs caught
Real games find what offline tests can't.

- **The Guardian's shift at the end of a turn.** When Combust's end-of-turn damage breaks Mode Shift, the game queues the Guardian's 20 block behind the monsters' turn start, so the block lasts into your next turn. The engine added it at once and lost it with the turn. Found in run 8's boss fight, fixed in the engine the same morning.

- **Perfected Strike played by Havoc.** Played from your hand, Perfected Strike counts itself as a Strike. Played by Havoc it is in limbo and doesn't. The engine always counted it and predicted 21 damage where the game dealt 18. Fixed in the engine: it counts the card only when it was played from the hand.

- **Regeneration heals first.** The game puts Regeneration's heal at the top of its queue, so at the end of a turn it heals before Combust takes its HP. The engine did it the other way round, and near max HP the two disagreed by one point (game 84, engine 85). Fixed in the engine today: Regeneration and Orichalcum now act first at the end of a turn.

- **Blood for Blood after Sharp Hide.** The Guardian's Sharp Hide hit for 3 as Blood for Blood+ was played. In the game the card is in limbo while that lands, so its own cost doesn't drop; the engine thought it was 1 cheaper, the play was refused, and the fallback rule lost run 16's boss fight from 75 HP. The controller now reads the cost the game shows, and the engine's rule for a card in limbo is queued for a fix.

- **Liquid Memories after Rampage.** The AI played Rampage, then drank Liquid Memories within the same second to take it back at cost 0. The card was still flying to the discard pile, and landing reset its cost, so the game refused the play. The controller now trusts a "free" mark only when the game shows the card at cost 0, and remembers which free copies it played.

- **Skill Potion's free card.** A Skill Potion gave Seeing Red at cost 0 for the turn. The card isn't in the deck list, so the controller had no base cost to compare. Cards made in a fight that the game shows at cost 0 are now marked free.

- **Snecko Oil.** The potion rolls every card's cost for the fight. The controller copied the engine's own roll instead of the game's, so the plan asked for a 0-cost Strike the game didn't have. Cards now carry the cost the game shows.

- **Rampage forgot its damage in a shuffle.** Rampage gains 5 damage each time it is played. The mark followed the pile, not the card, so a reshuffle dropped it and the engine predicted 8 damage where the game dealt 13. The mark now follows the game's own card id.

- **Havoc into Armaments.** Havoc played the top card, Armaments, and the game opened its pick, which the engine's plan (built on a different random top card) had no answer for. The controller now takes the sampled outcome that matches the game's cards.

- **The rules version moved.** The day's engine rule fixes changed the rules version to f59b662a, with five new recorded test games that pin each fix down.

- **Runic Dome.** It hides every enemy intent, and without intents the controller can't rebuild a fight. The map rules now skip it as a boss relic.

From the bridge's own write-up of today's runs, `docs/research/real-game-runs.md`.

What's next

## What the live runs added: a decision and nine backlog entries

Each backlog entry says what it would change, how likely it is to work, what it costs, and the cheapest probe that would kill it fast.

#### Decided · Ascension 20 becomes the training default

The owner chose A20 for training from here on. At A0 the real game is too easy for the fighter (22 of the 24 Act 1 bosses it reached, in 27 runs); at A20 it wins about half, so that is where play has room to grow. Training, labels and fine-tunes will play A20; A0 and A20 data are never pooled; the main test moves to A20 once A20 baselines exist. First the training scripts need an ascension setting and the datasets need to record it.

#### 82 · Never pass an immediate win

Once a fight has cost a lot of HP, the search can score carrying on above killing the last monster: run 56 left a Blue Slaver at 6 HP from turn 7 to turn 18. Cap an unfinished position's score at the best finish its HP allows. The bridge's fight guard covers it live meanwhile. Rated likely.

#### 83 · Price each fight for this deck

The map prices every hallway fight at 15 HP and every elite at 40, for any deck, so a weak deck walks into Lagavulin. Use the fight net, plus a few trial fights for close calls, to price each fight for the deck and HP at hand: a separate run layer that uses the fighter as a tool. Rated even; under half a CPU-hour to test.

#### 84 · Search the promising moves first

The owner's idea. When a two-turn search hits its node cap, the moves cut are whichever come last in hand order. Expand them in the order the one-turn search ranks them, and prune chance outcomes that can't change the choice. Rated even for better play, likely for speed.

#### 85 · Break stalls

Run 83 fought four gremlins for 84 turns: within two turns, killing the Shield Gremlin that kept re-blocking its ally never looked worth it. Make progress visible to the score, or as a stopgap hand a fight past about 30 turns to the fallback. Rated likely.

#### 86 · Check the chance steps too

Today's match rates skip any step with chance in it. Once the game shows a draw or a roll, replay the engine with that outcome forced and compare the rest, as its own count. Rated likely to surface monster-turn differences the count hides now.

#### 87 · Work out card rewards early

Low priority, the owner's idea. The game's card reward is fixed by its seed before the fight starts, so the bridge could compute the pick while the game animates. A cache only: the fight never reads it. About 5% of a run's time to save.

#### 79 · Answer every pick from the game

When a random card opens a pick (Havoc into Armaments, Warcry, Distilled Chaos), the bridge answers from the closest of the engine's 32 predicted samples, because the engine can't rebuild a fight paused at a pick. Teach it to, and every pick comes from the game's own state. Rated likely; engine work, no CPU.

#### 80 · Ascension 20 for the net

The net has never trained at A20, and its first A20 Slime Boss losses arrived with 49 to 64 HP and lost up to 33 HP in a single monster turn. First size the gap with the test run at A20; then A20 in training or in the card picker's trial fights; and a run layer that weighs HP, plus gold, which the bot never spends. Rated even.

#### 81 · Show the net the whole schedule

The owner's idea. The net sees each monster's intent on show and nothing after it, so a threat past the search horizon, like Hexaghost's turn-9 Inferno, is invisible. Give it the schedule ahead: turns until the biggest hit, its damage, Strength gained before it, the place in the cycle. Rated even; about 3 CPU-hours to test.

Statistics

## 23 days in numbers, period by period

Behind the scenes (nets trained, practice fights, the job queue, the code, the writing) and on the table (what it picks, plays and thinks hardest about in the real game). The same eight stretches as the story, plus a section across the whole project. Every number names its setting, and each was recomputed by a second, independent script before it went here. 

What the records point at

It doesn't always take the kill, and some fights stall.

Three of the longest fights below are stalls, not hard fights. In A20 run 83 a Shield Gremlin on 4 HP kept re-blocking a Mad Gremlin for 84 turns, because striking the shielded one scored a hair higher (1.51 against 1.49). In A0 run 18 an Acid Slime sat on 4 HP for about 23 turns, because every option, killing it included, scored the same. In A20 run 56 a Blue Slaver sat on 6 HP for turns 7 to 18 while the fight cost 70 HP, because carrying on scored above the kill. Two backlog entries take this on (What's next): 82, never pass an immediate win, and 85, make progress count so a fight can't stall. Meanwhile the bridge's fight guard takes a winning move the search passes, from run 58 on.

76 statistics. Each was mined by one script and recomputed by an independent second script; "corrected" means the check changed it. Each names its setting; numbers from different settings are not comparable.

### Across the whole project (23 October to 14 November)

**521 nets in 23 days, and the kind of training flipped twice** [project history · all 23 days]

The project trained 521 value nets that were saved with weights. Up to the end of period 4 almost all were trained from scratch (285 of 331). In periods 5 and 6 short fine-tunes of an existing net took over (145 of 153). In period 7 the new content brought back training from scratch (20 of 29). The busiest stretch was period 3, the first three days with the owner away: 155 nets, about 52 a day.

- Setting: Every runs/2026-* folder that holds a model.pt. 'Fine-tune' means the config names a parent net or a games count (scripts/finetune.py and family.py bursts). 'From scratch' means the config has n_train: scripts/learn.py in rounds of self-play, or the single-shot baseline trainings of 10-24 (P1, no rounds). Dated by created in run.json (local time). · n: 521 nets (P1 8, P2 112, P3 155, P4 56, P5 17, P6 136, P7 29, P8 8)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Five more run folders have no saved model (unfinished or failed) and are not counted. Periods differ in length (2 to 4 days); per day, P3 is still the busiest. The 8 P1 nets were single trainings, not rounds of self-play. The longest single training job was a fine-tune on 11-07 (0278-finetune, 3.95 hours on 3 threads); the one that used the most machine time was allcontent40k-s101 on 11-08 (3.4 hours on 6 threads, about 20 thread-hours).

**4.4 million practice fights, a quarter of them on one day** [project history · all 23 days]

The engine played 4,370,543 distinct practice fights to make training data, stored in 268 distinct datasets. 31 October alone produced 1,132,230 fights, more than periods 1, 2, 5, 6 and 8 together (1,082,768). Most of the data (3.57 million fights, 82%) came from round-0 pools that the search played with its hand-written heuristic. Only 0.80 million fights were played by a net, which is self-play in the strict sense.

- Setting: Every datasets/<id>/manifest.json (n_games), counted once per distinct games file (games_sha256). This drops 17 byte-identical regenerated copies (104,069 fights) and the leftover daf87b2b715e.tmp folder. Each dataset is dated by the first run that trained on it; datasets no run used are dated by their file time. · n: 268 distinct datasets, 4,370,543 fights
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: A dataset counts once, on the day it was first used, even if later nets trained on it again. Copies whose games file is identical to an earlier one (the same seeds regenerated) are counted only once. Pools played by the heuristic are cheap and large (up to 240,000 fights). Sets played by a net are small (a few thousand per round).

**How long a job waited in the queue before it started** [project history · all 23 days]

In period 5 the median job started 5.5 minutes after it was queued. In period 6, when family chains queued hundreds of reads at once, the median wait rose to 124 minutes, and one job in ten waited more than 4.7 hours. The longest wait in the queue's history was 30.6 hours: 0059l8b-bash, queued 11-01 15:48 and started 11-02 22:21. It was waiting for memory, not for another job: its 6 GB reservation never fit in the free RAM, and it started one minute after a manager cut the reservation to 3.5 GB.

- Setting: started jobs; wait = started minus added; period by start time · n: 1,091 started jobs (P3 105, P4 168, P5 120, P6 470, P7 182, P8 46)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Part of each wait is deliberate. About 3 in 10 started jobs were queued to run only after another job (--after) or only if another passed (--if-passed). Others waited for their memory reservation to fit, so a wait does not only mean waiting for free threads. Periods 1 and 2 had no queue.

**The machine did not sleep: busy threads by hour of day** [project history · all 23 days]

The queue ran day and night. Between midnight and 08:00, it used 31.5% of all queue thread-hours, close to the 33.3% an even spread would give. Over the whole queue the quietest hour, 07:00, still averaged 3.7 busy threads against 5.5 at the busiest, 10:00. In period 3 the machine was busy around the clock (12 threads at midnight, 01:00 and 08:00). In period 5 the nights were the quiet part (about 2 threads, 1.6 to 2.3, from 22:00 to 04:00), while the long mutated-monster jobs ran in the day.

- Setting: started jobs; each job's threads spread over the clock hours it ran; value = average busy threads in that clock hour for that period (thread-hours divided by the number of such hours the queue existed, 10-30 12:00 to 11-14 06:00) · n: 1,091 started jobs, 1,710 thread-hours
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Threads are what each job declared, not measured CPU use. Work run outside the queue (training in periods 1 and 2, the real game, builds) is not counted. P3 covers only 10-30 12:00 to 10-31, and P8 ends with the last finished job on 11-14 06:07, so each of their cells averages over one or two days (P8's 07:00 to 23:00 cells come from 11-13 alone).

**Why jobs failed, period by period** [project history · all 23 days]

Of 1,224 jobs, 1,031 ended ok, 131 were cancelled before they started (20 of them automatically, because a job they depended on failed), 53 failed and 9 were killed, lost or ended without a status. The most common cause of failure was a missing file or module (14), often a job pinned to a code snapshot that lacked a file it needed. A test run found a fault 11 times (10 full-suite runs and one single test file), 6 of them in period 6. Out of memory struck in two bursts: three self-play learn jobs in period 3 and three in period 8, when the wide recipe's re-runs died one after another (0987, 0988, 0997) right after 0975 died of a stack overflow.

- Setting: jobs/done with status failed; cause read by hand from each job's log tail, reason field and exit code; period by start time (added time for two jobs that never started) · n: 53 failed jobs (P3 10, P4 4, P5 7, P6 11, P7 9, P8 12)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The causes are a hand classification of the last error lines in each log, so a few could belong to a neighbouring group (0057a6i shares its log file with another job, so its cause can't be checked). A failed test run counts as a job doing its job. The 'refused' failures are the gauntlet's own guard stopping a comparison with a differently-played control, which is also a check working as intended. Most cells hold 0 to 3 jobs; read the grid as where failures happened, not as rates.

From 1 Nov the 11-floor gauntlet took over (347 files), and from period 5 the mutated benches (167 files). Period 6, when real Act 1 went in, was the busiest stretch: 412 result files and training runs, against 32 in the final two days.

- Setting: Training runs from runs/index.csv (by run date; 498 of the 535 run folders are indexed); ladder tests from results/ladder/log.jsonl; result files (.json, top level only) in results/<kind>/ by file modification time. 'other' = exact-puzzles, adapt, packs, gen_powers, depth_probe. Real-game files and smaller kinds (r0-scores, bosses, power_exposure, rollout, baseline, warm-start and others, about 40 files) are left out. · n: 1,206 measurements (498 runs, 39 ladder tests, 669 result files)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Result files are dated by modification time, so a file rewritten later counts in the later period. One file can hold 500 fights or 20; this counts files, not fights. results/ is not in git, so there is no commit date to cross-check. Files in subfolders and minor result kinds are not counted.

**The cell gauntlet's best barely moved after the first week of November** [project history · all 23 days]

On the cell gauntlet (11 floors, one-turn search, the first 500 fight sequences), the best clear rate any player reached rose from 0.646 in period 4 to 0.682 in period 7, and the typical read (median) from 0.612 to 0.648. For scale: the hand-written heuristic reads 0.596, and the 10-29 net, the one the product kept using, reads 0.634. The record 0.682 came from one training seed of the genpack40k recipe (a rerun gave the identical result); its sister seeds read 0.642 and 0.676. Period 8 has only 2 reads.

Periods are dated from the result files' modification times because results/ is not in git. The second block (sequences 500-999, the -b500 files) is easier (the 10-29 net reads 0.686 there against 0.634 here) and is kept off this chart. The best of many reads is pulled upward by luck: seeds of one recipe differ by up to 0.05. Players differ in leaf options (plain or @tsl1).

**Lines of Rust and Python, day by day** [project history · all 23 days]

Python overtook Rust on 27 Oct and pulled far ahead while the lab ran experiments, ending at 68,068 lines. Rust grew in bursts: the rules-engine series on 1-2 Nov (16,316 to 20,158), almost nothing while the AI roles took over (20,158 to 20,595 over 3-5 Nov), then real Act 1 content, relics, potions and the real-game bridge, which took it from 25,890 on 9 Nov to 49,600 on 14 Nov.

- Setting: every .rs and every .py file in main's tree (engine, binding crate, solved-position DB, scripts, tests) at the last main commit before local midnight each day · n: 2,591 commits, 23 days
- Check: corrected by its independent check

**The rules version held for 21 days, then moved five times in one day** [project history · all 23 days]

Every dataset and run is stamped with a rules version: a hash of 9,000 seeded random games recorded on 24 Oct (golden.txt). It stayed 7042d1d6 for three weeks: a 2 Nov change was undone 9 minutes later, and a 10 Nov rewrite was declared the same rules. Then the real game exposed rule gaps, and the version moved five times between 01:08 and 22:56 on 14 Nov. The first move rewrote 139 of the 9,000 games (random-spot card inserts). Each of the other four added cover games for rules fixed that day: 5 at once (turn-end order, Duplication, Guardian, Feed and others), then one each for Lagavulin's wake, Pocketwatch and Fire Breathing on a Curse.

- Setting: commits that changed engine/tests/data/golden.txt; hash as scripts/registry.py rules_version computes it · n: 9 commits touching golden.txt; 5 real version moves
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The hash only moves when a rule change alters a golden game. Several rule fixes (Perfected Strike, Mode Shift block, turn-end order of Burn and Metallicize, and others) left golden unchanged until the cover games of 14 Nov were added, so 'rules version unchanged' did not mean 'rules unchanged'. Times are author times; for the four later commits on 14 Nov, the commit times are 4 to 16 minutes later.

**Engine speed changes, each measured before vs after** [project history · all 23 days]

The biggest speed-up came on 25 Oct, when the net's forward pass moved from Python to Rust: self-play went from 23.5 to 179 games per second (Python on 4 threads vs Rust on all threads, so part of the gain is threads). Later work moved speed both ways. On 2 Nov the new rule machinery cost one-turn search 7.5% at 1 thread, and the same day a search memo that stores each state once gained it back 52%. On 6 Nov, shrinking the combat state from 1,292 to 744 bytes added 17.6%.

- Setting: speed checks recorded in docs/decisions.md and the sparse-piles spec; each event is its own before-vs-after measurement on the laptop, with its own workload · n: 5 measured changes; 3 to 22 interleaved runs each
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Each check used its own workload and machine load, so the numbers are not on one scale and cannot be added up. The 25 Oct figure compares different thread counts. The 13 Nov jobs crashed out of memory partway through.

Chart (timeline) data:

| When | Event | value |
|---|---|---|
| 10-25 | Rust inference: self-play 23.5 to 179 games/s (7.6x; Python on 4 threads vs Rust on all threads, median of 5) | – |
| 11-02 | Rules-engine series (specs 2-9): one-turn search -7.5% at 1 thread, -13.7% at all threads; state grew 1,028 to 1,268 bytes | – |
| 11-02 | Search memo stores each state once: one-turn search +52% at 1 thread, +32% at all threads (5 runs) | – |
| 11-06 | Sparse state: 1,292 to 744 bytes; search +17.6% at 1 thread, +35.5% at all threads (11 runs) | – |

**Who wrote the decisions, period by period** [project history · all 23 days]

docs/decisions.md holds 499 dated entries. While the owner sat at the desk (periods 1-2), every entry came from their session (25). From 10-29 Claude wrote 125 entries marked 'owner away' in five days. From 11-03 the AI roles took over and wrote 213 entries marked 'on the owner's behalf', 97 of them in period 6 alone. The owner signed 55 entries from 10-29 on (8 of them tagged 'owner present', 10-31 to 11-02): 1 in period 3, then 10-14 per period in periods 4-7 and 5 in the two-day period 8. Period 6 (11-06..11-09) was the busiest stretch with 149 entries. The busiest single day was 11-02, with 55.

- Setting: Every '## ' heading in docs/decisions.md at HEAD. Date = git blame author time of the heading line (local time). Who = the heading's own tag. A tag starting '(owner' or containing 'owner present', or an untagged entry before 10-29, counts as owner. '(owner away...)' counts as Claude while the owner was away, and '(on the owner's behalf...)' as the roles. 'Lane X opened/closed' headings are lane records. The rest are untagged build notes; half of those (11 of 22) are the rules-engine series built overnight on 11-02, 01:36-05:26. · n: 499 entries
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The tag is the writer's own label, not a check of who typed it. Entries marked 'owner present' were likely typed by Claude with the owner at the desk. Blame dates an entry by the last change to its heading line, so a retitled entry moves to the day of the retitle. Periods differ in length (2 to 4 days).

**Hexaghost and The Guardian were the run-killers** [project history · all 23 days]

Across 58,546 lost gauntlet runs (duplicate files removed), Hexaghost ended 16,962 (29%), The Guardian 12,545 (21%) and Slime Boss 6,581 (11%). Together the three bosses ended 62% of all failed runs. In P4 to P7 a player who reached Hexaghost lost to him 33% to 38% of the time. In P8 The Guardian edged ahead, 28.7% against 27.1%. Slime Boss cost 10% to 14%, and each of the three elites only 3% to 6% of the fights against it.

- Setting: 335 gauntlet files with distinct sequences (12 copy or rerun files dropped), all players mixed, one shape and rest 0.3; share of fights against each encounter that were lost, per period · n: P4 302,414 fights, P5 159,071, P6 815,731, P7 438,870, P8 50,250; Hexaghost reached 47,950 times
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Each period mixes different players, decks and nets, so change across periods is not a measure of progress. P8 is small, about 1,400 fights per boss. The boss comes after a fixed rest, but elites come at whatever HP is left, so their lower loss rate partly reflects when they appear. Copies of the same results (sanity, thread and scale checks, a rerun) are counted once.

### Period 2 (P2): Self-play, with the owner at the desk (25 to 28 October)

**The first ruler: hard fights, with unseen cards held out** [project history · 25 to 28 October]

Before the gauntlet existed, nets were scored on a 'tight' bench of hard single fights. The bench had two halves: decks built from the training card pool, and decks that hold cards the net never trained with (held out). From 25 to 28 Oct, 22 runs were scored on it. The best held-out win rate was 0.511 (score+branch, seed 1, 10 rounds, 28 Oct), against 0.460 for the 24 Oct baseline. That best result's 95% range is 0.47-0.55 over 550 games, and it is the top of 22 tries, so the gain is modest. Runs that won more with training-pool decks also won more with held-out-card decks (correlation 0.86): a run's ranking carried over to cards it had not seen. The bench last appears in the run index on 28 Oct; after that the project moved to the ladder and the gauntlet.

- Setting: runs/index.csv rows with bench_version 1 dated 25-28 Oct; tight bench win rate, train half (training card pool) vs held_out half (decks with held-out cards) · n: 22 training runs; 550 held-out games per run
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Win rates on this bench are not comparable with the gauntlet's clear rates: it scores single hard fights, not 11-floor sequences. The two halves use the same encounters and the same net, so part of the 0.86 correlation is shared fight difficulty, not proof the net generalizes. Each held-out rate rests on 550 games (about +/-0.04), and 0.511 is the best of 22 runs.

**The last turn is easy; one turn before it is not** [project history · 25 to 28 October]

On 10-26 an exact solver (up to 1 million positions per puzzle, every random event listed) gave the true value of every option in 2,000 end-of-fight decisions. The heuristic's one-turn search picked an exactly best option on all 1,552 last-turn puzzles, but on 9 to 10% of the puzzles one or more turns before the end it picked worse, giving up about 3 HP (0.03 score) on average there. The project's nets of that day scored the same as the heuristic within error: 13 of the 448 deeper puzzles fooled all four evaluators.

- Setting: Heuristic evaluator, one-turn search (limit 16) against exact values; tight-fight puzzles, train and held-out halves pooled (1,000 each). Horizon = turns left in the fight. · n: 2,000 puzzles (1,552 last turn, 313 one turn out, 135 two or more out)
- Check: confirmed by an independent check
- How it was counted: End-of-fight decisions only, from games the heuristic itself played; 77% of puzzles are last-turn ones where any evaluator scores 100% by construction. The 'all four evaluators' count comes from docs/research/exact-puzzles.md, the rest from the heuristic's result file.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | % best pick | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Last turn (horizon 0) | 100 | 1,552 of 1,552; one turn of search sees the end of the fight |
| One turn before the end | 90.1 | 282 of 313; mean regret 0.033 (about 3 HP) |
| Two or more turns before | 91.1 | 123 of 135; mean regret 0.027 |
| Contested (best beats 2nd by 0.05+) | 92.7 | 240 of 259; mean regret 0.037 |

**2,000 endgame puzzles, and what the right move is worth** [project history · 25 to 28 October]

On 26 October the exact solver turned 2,000 near-the-end positions from close fights into puzzles and worked out the true value of every move. In 1,111 of them the fight was already lost whatever you played, and in 133 it was won whatever you played. In 97 puzzles exactly one move out of three or more wins for certain and every other move loses for certain. The biggest knife edge has nine moves against three Louses at 7 HP, and only Clothesline+ on the second target wins. In one Lots of Slimes puzzle plain Thunderclap wins and Thunderclap+ loses. The likely reason is order: playing the plain one first means its Vulnerable makes the upgraded one hit harder.

- Setting: data/bench/puzzles.json: 1,000 train and 1,000 held-out puzzles, one per tight-fight game played by search plus the hand-written player on fresh seeds; exact values from sts.solve with a 1M-node budget; a loss chance within 1e-9 of 0 or 1 counts as certain · n: 2,000 puzzles (1,552 on the fight's last turn, 448 one turn or more from the end)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The positions come from close fights played by the hand-written player, so lost positions are over-represented. A 'certain' loss or win means a loss chance of 1 or 0 under best play. The file stores 26 certain losses as 0.9999999999999998, so the counts use a 1e-9 tolerance. The Thunderclap order explanation is my reading of the card numbers (4 + 7x1.5 = 14 against 7 + 4x1.5 = 13); the solver doesn't record it.

Chart (tally) data:

| Item | puzzles |
|---|---|
| Lost whatever you play | 1,111 |
| The move changes the odds or the HP left | 659 |
| Won whatever you play | 133 |
| Only one of 3+ moves wins; all others lose for certain | 97 |

### Period 3 (P3): Claude runs the lab day to day (29 to 31 October)

**The product's net was trained on 10-29 and has held the product role since 11-02** [project history · 29 to 31 October]

The product's net was trained on the evening of 29 October. (The real game on 14 November was played by another net, pack net seed 201, trained from scratch on 10 November on games with the real Act 1 cards.) Its family tree is short. A 60,000-fight pool played by the heuristic trained a first net. That net's 5,775 fights trained a second, whose 6,013 fights made up round 2. The seed-check run then retrained round 2 from scratch at three training seeds, and seed 1 became the product net. It learned from all 11,788 self-play fights plus a random sixth of the examples from the heuristic pool. On 11-01 two fine-tunes of it (burst8, then burst8hp2) took over for a day. On 11-02 it beat them on HP kept at the end of a run and became the product net, and none of the 383 nets trained after it has replaced it since. 167 runs started from it, trained on data it played, or used it to play.

- Setting: Lineage from round.json parent_run and dataset manifest parent_model fields. Round-0 sampling from round.json window.round0_sample (1/6 for rounds 1 and up; seed_check.py retrains on the same window). Product history from configs/product.json 'previous' and the docs/decisions.md entries of 11-01 and 11-02. Later nets are runs with a model.pt whose run.json created time is after 10-29 22:32:39 (local time). · n: 1 product net; 11,788 self-play fights + 1/6 of the examples from a 60,000-fight heuristic pool (+3,000 validation fights); 383 later nets (including its 2 seed siblings); 167 runs that refer to it directly (173 counting their descendants)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Seeds 101 and 201 of the same retrain are its siblings and are counted among the 383. Most later nets were experiments judged on their own yardsticks, not challengers. 'Not replaced since 11-02' means none passed the rule for becoming the product, not that every one played worse. The 1/6 sample is of examples spread across all 60,000 pool fights, so the pool still shaped it.

**After the first net beat the heuristic, the next 17 challengers all failed to beat it** [project history · 29 to 31 October]

On 30 Oct the 10-29 net passed the paired ladder against the heuristic (+3.7 points of the same fights won, 1,150 pairs) and became champion. None of the next 17 candidates (30-31 Oct) beat it: its two seed twins, three nets trained on 40k positions, five lean-threshold seeds, three distilled nets, three cover21 nets and an ensemble. The best two were dead even (ensemble +0.2, seed twin s201 0.0). The cover21 nets did worst, about 6 to 7 points behind. The more-data nets (data40k) were 1.4 to 2.6 points behind, so more data did not help here. The streak ended on 1 Nov, when the burst8 nets passed against it.

- Setting: Paired ladder, one-turn net player: candidate and champion (the 10-29 net) play the same fight with the same seed; lead = candidate's fight wins minus champion's, per pair; a sequential test stops at a verdict (pass needs evidence of a 1-point gain) · n: 17 tests against the 10-29 net, 482 to 4,274 pairs each (30-31 Oct); plus the promoting test against the heuristic
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The test stops early once a verdict is clear, so big losses come from few pairs and their size is rough. A fail means no proof of a 1-point gain, not proof of a loss. The promoting +3.7 against the heuristic is a different comparison and is left off the bars; on its held-out half of fights the lead was only +1.0 (interval -1.7 to +3.8). The ladder measures one-turn play on single fights. The product later kept the 10-29 net because of whole-run end-HP reads, not because of this ladder (decisions 2026-11-02), and the ladder itself crowned an 11-01 net (burst8hp2) in the meantime.

**Two-turn search lost until it was allowed to think longer** [project history · 29 to 31 October]

In the first paired test of two-turn search (10-30), capped at 50,000 positions per decision, it won 4.8 points fewer elite and boss fights than plain one-turn search. Raising the cap to 400,000 turned that to +1.6. Making it fall back to the one-turn pick whenever the tree was still cut off (about 1 decision in 21 in play) gave +6.1. With the 10-29 net scoring the leaves, the same fixes moved it from -1.0 to +5.5. Most of the early loss came from cut-off trees, not from searching deeper.

- Setting: Held-out decks, elite and boss fights. Every row uses the same 1,336 fights, and the one-turn results are identical fight for fight within each leaf scorer. Two-turn limit 16, sample k 4. Value = two-turn minus one-turn win rate. · n: 1,336 paired fights per row (se 0.013 to 0.015)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The cut shares in the notes come from a small probe of every third decision (61 to 133 decisions per run). In full play at 400k, 4.7 to 4.8% of decisions were cut, mostly in Sentries and Slime Boss. The research doc's '1%' figure disagrees with its own per-encounter table. The net rows have no plain cap-400k run, so for them the cap increase and the fallback can't be separated. Lagavulin was still slightly negative (about -3) with the fallback. Thread counts differed between runs (6, 4, 6), which affects timing, not wins.

| Item | win-rate points, two-turn minus one-turn | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Heuristic leaves, cap 50k | -4.8 | probe: mean 11,535 positions per decision, 13.5% of probed decisions cut |
| Heuristic leaves, cap 400k | 1.6 | mean 76,372 positions, 16% of probed (hard) decisions cut |
| Heuristic leaves, cap 400k + fall back when cut | 6.1 | one-turn 49.8% -> two-turn 55.8%; 4.7% of decisions in play were cut |
| 10-29 net leaves, cap 50k | -1 | mean 16,341 positions, 23% of probed decisions cut |
| 10-29 net leaves, cap 400k + fall back | 5.5 | one-turn 53.5% -> two-turn 59.1%; 4.8% of decisions in play were cut |

**43 made-up Power cards: who coped better?** [project history · 29 to 31 October]

On 31 October the project built Power cards from building blocks and swapped each one in for Demon Form or Metallicize in held-out fights. The hand-written player won less with 31 of the 43 cards. The 29 October net lost less than the hand-written player with 21 cards and more with 14. The hand-written player's two worst cards were defensive ones whose payoff comes later. One was '1 energy: this turn, when you lose HP, give all enemies 1 Weak' (-0.31 wins for the hand-written player, -0.19 for the net). With '2 energy: deal 1 damage to all enemies at the end of this turn', a card that does almost nothing, the hand-written player lost 0.225 wins and the net lost 0.05.

- Setting: gen_powers bench: tight2 held-out cells, first 4 fights per cell, one turn deep. Each generated card replaces the Demon Form or Metallicize in the same fight (paired). Hand-written player vs the 29 October net (r2 s1). · n: 326 cells, 1,304 fights per set; 43 cards with 12 to 52 fights each
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Each card has only 12 to 52 fights, so chance alone moves a single card's point by about 0.1 wins. Read the cloud, not one dot. 8 cards tie (the point sits on the diagonal). Labels don't show how many copies of the card the deck holds (1 to 3). Over all 326 cells, the net's lead over the hand-written player grew by +0.021 (se 0.011) on generated cards. The hand-written player rates only Strength powers, so it is a weak yardstick on generated content.
