### Period 4 (P4): From single fights to an Act (1 to 2 November)

**More data helped, until 40,000 fights a group** [project history · 1 to 2 November]

The lab tested whether more training fights make a better net. Going from 5,000 to 20,000 fights per group raised the held-out won share by +0.011. Going from 20,000 to 40,000 added +0.009. Going from 40,000 to 80,000 (a 240,000-fight dataset, the biggest the project ever made) added only +0.003, and the two seeds disagreed (+0.024 and -0.019). On 1 November the lab stopped scaling data and kept the 20k default for experiments. 40k became the usual size for new from-scratch nets only from 8 November.

- Setting: Round-1 nets on the tight2 bench, one turn deep, held-out half (12,200 fights per net). Won-share differences are paired by seed. Only n_train differs between arms. · n: 5k vs 20k: 5 seeds; 20k vs 40k: 3 seeds; 40k vs 80k: 2 seeds
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The first step is a fourfold increase and the other two are doublings. With only 2 seeds at 80k, the flat result is weak evidence. Both 80k nets also failed the ladder against the 10-29 champion (-0.018, -0.024). The 80k training jobs took 2.9 hours (s1) and 1.0 hours (s101) on 6 threads. The 5k/20k/40k results were read on 31 October; the 80k results and the stop decision came on 1 November.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | held-out won share gained | Note |
|---|---|---|
| 5k to 20k fights per group | 0.011 | 5 seeds, se 0.006 |
| 20k to 40k | 0.009 | 3 seeds, se 0.003 |
| 40k to 80k | 0.003 | 2 seeds: +0.024 and -0.019 |

**The first card of a turn costs more than the rest together** [project history · 1 to 2 November]

Two-turn search re-plans after every card. On 11-02 a probe over 20 fights measured how big each search was: the turn's first decision explored 22,800 positions on average (0.34 s), the second 9,860, the third 4,270 and the fourth 1,430. The first decision took 61% of all positions searched. A follow-up on 11-03 reused the tree from the previous card instead of rebuilding it: 30% fewer positions (4.81 million down to 3.38 million), with the same pick on every compared decision (526).

- Setting: 10-29 net, two-turn search (limit 16, sample k 4, cap 400,000), 20 fights: 16 elite (Gremlin Nob, Lagavulin, Three Sentries) and 4 Slime Boss, 5 deck families; 502 searches over 139 turns. Positions = search tree nodes. · n: 502 searches (139 / 134 / 122 / 85 / 18 / 4 by place in turn)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: 20 fights (16 elite, 4 Slime Boss) over 5 deck families; milliseconds are wall time on the dev machine. The 5th and 6th rows rest on 18 and 4 searches. The tree-reuse figure is from results/depth/line-step-compare.json (same 20 fights, run just after midnight on 11-03).

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | positions searched per decision (mean) | Note |
|---|---|---|
| 1st decision of the turn | 22,801 | n 139; 4,101 leaves scored; 345 ms |
| 2nd | 9,859 | n 134; 169 ms |
| 3rd | 4,271 | n 122; 73 ms |
| 4th | 1,435 | n 85; 20 ms |
| 5th | 1,694 | n 18; small n |
| 6th | 145 | n 4; small n |

**Playing each option out 64 times saved 2 of 69 lost boss fights** [project history · 1 to 2 November]

On 11-02 the lab ran a ceiling probe: at every decision of a replayed fight, play each option to the end of the fight with the one-turn net and pick the best average. It replayed the fatal fight of the 117 sequences no player had cleared. With 16 playouts per option it won 3 of them, and with 64 it won 7 (6%). Only 2 of those 7 were among the 69 boss fights (0 at 16 playouts). The other 5 were elites or hallway fights. On 30 boss fights the product had won, the rollout player lost 7 (16 playouts) and 3 (64 playouts). With 64 playouts, a fight took on average 5,300 playouts and 7 s on the lost set, and 7,700 playouts and 52 s on the won set. The result was under the 10% bar, so the lab read it as little headroom left in the fatal fight: losses are decided earlier, by HP spent on the way and by the deck.

- Setting: Fatal fights of the 117 never-cleared sequences (69 floor-10 bosses, 48 earlier fights, mostly floor-9 elites), replayed at the entry HP of the product's run (champion-hpe0.01-two). Also 30 floor-10 boss fights the product won. Playout and decision player: 11-01 burst8hp2 net @hpb0.01, one turn deep. 4 threads, seed 0. Value: share of fights the rollout player won. · n: 69 lost boss fights, 48 lost non-boss fights, 30 won boss fights, each replayed at 16 and at 64 playouts per option
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The fights were chosen by their outcome, so part of each change comes from selection (regression to the mean), not from the rollouts. n is small: 2 boss rescues and 30 won fights. The rollout player is the one-turn net, which is weaker than the product on bosses (all 3 losses at 64 playouts were Hexaghost at 80 HP), so this sets a lower bound on headroom, not a ceiling. It replays only the fatal fight, not the whole run.

Chart (slope) data:

Values in % fights won by the rollout player.

| Item | 16 playouts per option | 64 playouts per option |
|---|---|---|
| Lost boss fights (69) | 0 | 2.9 |
| Lost elite and hallway fights (48) | 6.3 | 10.4 |
| Boss fights the product won (30) | 76.7 | 90 |

### Period 5 (P5): The AI roles take over (3 to 5 November)

**The three longest jobs ran 34 hours in one lane, far past their first estimates** [project history · 3 to 5 November]

The longest job in the queue's history is 0083-mutate-monsters: 13 h 56 min on 3 threads (11-04 03:24 to 17:21). It ran the two-turn product player on a stage-A table of mutated monsters, widened from 40x4 to 200x4. The second and third longest (0092, 10 h 11 min; 0113, 10 h 00 min) came from the same goal1-yardstick lane and followed it with gaps of 56 and 6 minutes. That lane kept 3 threads busy from 11-04 03:24 to 11-05 14:35. All three outran their first estimates (340, 450 and 330 min) by 1.4x to 2.5x. The steward raised each estimate mid-run from the job's pace, the last time when the job was 97-99% done. Short jobs (under an hour) usually finished in about half their estimate (median 0.50x, n=954).

- Setting: jobs that started and ended; wall time from started to ended; first estimate = the 'from' of the first entry in the job's estimates list, or estimate_min if it was never revised · n: 1,091 started jobs; the top 3 shown
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Wall time includes any slowdown from other jobs sharing the machine (other lanes ran alongside). The stored estimate_min is the last estimate, revised from pace near the end of each run, so comparing against it makes the estimates look accurate when they weren't. Three jobs is too few to say that long jobs are estimated worse in general.

**Seventeen times the thinking for less than one HP** [project history · 3 to 5 November]

On 11-04 the lab tested whether searching deeper than two turns pays off on floor-9 elites. A third turn of search (cap 1.6 million positions) took 44.5 s per fight, about 17 times the two-turn product's 2.5 s, and saved 0.75 HP per fight (se 0.81; all 48 fights won either way). A rollout leaf (16 playouts per option, about 1,160 playouts per fight, 10.4 s) lost 0.67 HP more per fight than the product (se 0.31) and lost 4 more fights out of 498. Neither reached the 1.5 HP bar, so the depth family was closed: on this laptop, searching past two turns stopped paying.

- Setting: Floor-9 elite fights from the headline control's runs. Each fight is paired against the two-turn product's own play on the same fight. Player: the 10-29 net @hpe0.01. The three-turn arm ran on the first 48 fights only. The two-turn k 4 and k 16 points come from an 11-07 rerun of the same 498 fights. · n: 498 elite fights (three-turn arm: 48)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The two-turn timings come from the 11-07 rerun of the same 498 fights (files two4 and two16). The two4 rerun reproduces the product's play exactly, fight for fight. The k 16 point is a paired difference: 40.65 against 40.31 HP left, +0.34 HP, se 0.16. It came after the 11-04 decision and changes search width, not depth. Rollout seconds are summed per fight. The three-turn arm's 48 fights recorded 130 cap cuts over 1,027 decisions.

#### The 1,000-run test of the product

**The net's favourite and least favourite cards** [simulated test · runs 0–999]

Offered Clash, the product took it 58% of the time, more than any other card; Spot Weakness, Anger and Inflame were also taken more often than not. At the bottom, Body Slam was taken only 5% of the time and Bloodletting 9%.

- Setting: Simulated headline runs 0-999, product (the 10-29 net searching two turns deep), curated Act 1 test cards; 9,903 card rewards, 1,401 skipped (14%) · n: 9,903 card rewards over 1,000 runs; each card offered 442-1,239 times
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: These are the curated test cards, not the real game's card pool. Some cards (FTL, Metallicize, Demon Form, Uppercut, Iron Wave and others) can only be offered in the held-out half of the runs, so they were offered about 440-530 times instead of about 1,150-1,240. Each reward offers three cards, so a card's take rate also depends on what it was offered next to. FTL is the Defect's card (0 cost, 5 damage, draw 1 if you've played fewer than 3 cards this turn), added to the Ironclad's test pool; it is not Flash of Steel.

**Demon Form: the hand-written player loves it, the net doesn't** [simulated test · runs 0–499]

The biggest disagreement between the two players is Demon Form. The hand-written player took it 68% of the time it was offered; the net took it 20% of the time. On almost every other card the two players' take rates were within 10 points of each other.

- Setting: Simulated headline runs 0-499, product (net, two turns deep) against the hand-written player (one turn deep), on the same seeded runs; cards offered at least 30 times to each · n: Demon Form offered 250 times to the product and 238 times to the hand-written player; 4,958 and 4,812 rewards in all
- Check: confirmed by an independent check
- How it was counted: Demon Form is a held-out card: the net never saw it in training. The hand-written player's runs that took Demon Form cleared 82 of 140 times (59%). Only 15 of its runs saw Demon Form offered and never took it, too few to compare against. The two players reach different decks and HP, so the same seed can give different offers later in a run.

**Picks that go with more clears** [simulated test · runs 0–999]

Of the runs where Metallicize was offered, the runs where the net took it cleared 93% of the time, against 82% when it didn't. At the other end, runs that took Bloodletting cleared 67% of the time, against 80% when they passed on it. This is a correlation, not a cause: a run that can afford Metallicize may already be going well.

- Setting: Simulated headline runs 0-999, product (net, two turns deep); for each card, only runs where it was offered at least once; at least 40 runs on each side; every card whose gap is 5 points or more is shown; overall clear rate 80.7% · n: 1,000 runs; for each shown card, 58-520 runs took it and 152-657 did not
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: This is a correlation only. The net picks according to the state of the run, so a weak run may grab Bloodletting or Body Slam out of need. A gap of about 5 points is within noise for a card taken in 100-150 runs, so the rows near plus or minus 5 (Anger, Twin Strike, Pommel Strike) are not meaningful on their own. Each run picks several cards, so the rows overlap. A card counts as "taken" if the run took it on any floor where it was offered.

**Each player's nemesis boss** [simulated test · runs 0–999]

Slime Boss is barely a threat to the net, which won 96% of its arrivals; Hexaghost (78%) and The Guardian (81%) are about level for it. The Guardian was the hand-written player's nightmare: it won only 52% of its fights against him. On the same seeds, the net beat him 86% of the time.

- Setting: Simulated headline runs; product (net, two turns deep) runs 0-999; hand-written player (one turn deep) runs 0-499; fights started on the boss floor (floor 11 of 11). Both players see identical encounters on the same seed. · n: Product: 947 boss arrivals; hand-written: 401 boss arrivals; 389 seeds (0-499) where both reached the boss
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The two players were tested on different run counts (1,000 against 500), and the weaker player reaches the boss with a weaker deck, so this is not a pure boss-skill comparison. The net's Hexaghost-vs-Guardian gap is within noise: on runs 0-499 it is 82% vs 81%, and on runs 500-999 it is 73% vs 81%. Same seeds where both players reached the boss: Guardian 112/130 (net) vs 67/130 (hand-written); Hexaghost 109/127 vs 80/127; Slime Boss 131/132 vs 111/132.

**How runs end** [simulated test · runs 0–999]

81% of the net's runs clear the act. Of the ones that don't, nearly three in four die to the boss (140 of 193), and most of the rest die to the second elite on floor 9. The hand-written player clears only 53% of the time, dies to the boss about twice as often and dies in hallway fights about four times as often.

- Setting: Simulated headline runs, 11-fight test act (hallways, elites on floors 6 and 9, boss on 11); product (net, two turns deep) runs 0-999; hand-written player (one turn deep) runs 0-499 · n: 1,000 product runs, 500 hand-written runs
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: This is the curated 11-fight test act, not a real Act 1 map: there are no shops or events, and the rests are simulated. Neither player died on floors 1-5. The net's cleared runs ended with 34 HP on average (lowest 7), against 30 for the hand-written player (lowest 7). The two players differ in both the evaluator (net vs hand-written) and the search depth (two turns vs one), so the gap can't be put down to either one alone.

Jaw Worm, Cultist, the louse packs, Fungi Beasts and Small Slimes went a combined 4,394 fights without killing the net once. Among non-boss fights, Lagavulin was the deadliest, but it still lost only 16 of 668.

- How it was counted: These are the curated test monsters, played as fixed 'easy' and 'hard' hallway slots. Where a fight sits in the run matters: elites on floor 9 start with less HP than on floor 6 (54 vs 74 on average) and account for 34 of the 36 elite losses. ### Period 6 (P6): Real Act 1 goes in (6 to 9 November)

**Fine-tunes on as few as 11 fights** [project history · 6 to 9 November]

In period 6 the lab mostly stopped training nets from scratch. Instead it fine-tuned one existing net in short bursts on new content's fights: 127 fine-tunes against 8 from-scratch runs. Of the 127 bursts, 94 used 99 fights or fewer, and 12 used just 11. The question had shifted from 'what is the best net?' to 'how few fights does the net need to adapt to new content?'.

- Setting: Fine-tune runs created 11-06 to 11-09 (run.json created, local time) whose run.json has a parent net; the games value from each config.json. One memory-check rerun with an identical config (ycards-heur-g400-s1-memcheck) is left out. · n: 127 fine-tune nets (one parent net); 8 from-scratch runs in the same days
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Burst sizes count fights of new content mixed into a 10,000-position replay pool, not the whole training set. The from-scratch runs in the same days used 5,000 or 40,000 training positions, a different unit, so the page doesn't compare the two. Small bursts are cheap, so many seeds and variants were run; that partly explains the counts.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | nets |
|---|---|
| 11 fights | 12 |
| 33 fights | 44 |
| 50 fights | 2 |
| 99 fights | 36 |
| 100 fights | 3 |
| 400 fights | 19 |
| 1,600 fights | 5 |
| 4,000 fights | 6 |

**Retraining the same recipe with a new seed moves the score as much as most ideas did** [project history · 6 to 9 November]

On 6-7 Nov the lanes read 9 training recipes, each trained with 3 to 5 different seeds, on the same 500 gauntlet sequences. For three recipes they also read the same nets a second way (@tsl1). Seeds of the same recipe differed by 1.4 to 5.2 points of clear rate (median 2.0). The 10-29 net's whole lead over the heuristic on these sequences is 3.8 points (0.634 vs 0.596). So the luck of a single training seed could make an idea look like it gained or lost most of that lead. This is why the project read seed twins before believing a result.

- Setting: Cell gauntlet (seed 20261101, rest 0.3), one-turn search, sequences 0-499, 500 sequences per read. Recipes with 3 or more training seeds whose reads all fall on 6-9 Nov (data20k was trained 10-31 and burst8 on 11-01; both were re-read 11-06/07). Rows marked @tsl1 are the same nets read with the tsl1 search option. · n: 9 recipes, 29 nets, 35 reads (11 recipe-and-read groups)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Reading the same net again gives exactly the same result, so the gap comes from the training seed. But how big it is depends on which 500 sequences are used: on sequences 500-999 the data20k seeds differ by 3.4 points, not 5.2. With only 3 seeds the gap understates the full range. On this gauntlet, across all 24 recipe-and-read groups with 2 or more seeds from every period, the median gap is 1.6 points.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | gap between best and worst seed (clear-rate points) | Note |
|---|---|---|
| data20k (5 seeds) | 5.2 | 0.586 to 0.638 |
| ycards heur-g400 | 3.4 | 0.600 to 0.634 |
| burst8 'neither' | 3.2 | 0.588 to 0.620 |
| ycards net-g1600, read @tsl1 | 3 | 0.638 to 0.668 (same nets as the row below) |
| ycards net-g400 | 2.6 | 0.596 to 0.622 |
| ycards net-g1600 | 2 | 0.628 to 0.648 |
| burst8, read @tsl1 | 1.6 | 0.646 to 0.662 |

| ycards two-g400 | 1.4 | 0.614 to 0.628 |

**22 new Ironclad cards the net had never seen** [project history · 6 to 9 November]

When the real Act 1 Ironclad cards went in on 7 November, the 29 October net was tested on 22 cards it had never trained on, with three copies of each card put into the deck. On 19 of them it lost nothing against the hand-written player: 18 read level, and with Berserk it pulled ahead (+0.066 wins). Berserk made both players worse, but the hand-written player got worse by more, and it played Berserk on only 15% of the turns it held one. Three cards cost the net: Impervious (-0.122 wins), Disarm (-0.118) and Intimidate (-0.062). All three are Exhaust skills whose whole value is a number the net must read: 30 Block, negative enemy Strength, Weak on every enemy.

- Setting: tight2 held-out elite and boss cells (126 elite, 41 boss), three fights each, three copies of the card in place of three Strikes (then Defends), paired with the same fights without the card; 29 October net @hpb0.01 one turn deep (@tsl1 on decks with a turn-start power) minus the hand-written player · n: 167 cells x 3 fights per card, 22 cards; se 0.026 to 0.034 wins
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: With 22 cards read at 2 se, about one false hit is expected by chance. Disarm and Impervious are well past that; Intimidate and Berserk sit right at 2 se, and Berserk's gain comes from the 41 boss cells alone. 'Why' (inputs outside the training range) is the lane's hypothesis, not a measurement: Shockwave also applies Weak and reads level. A lead measures the net against the hand-written player, not whether the card is good (Burning Pact and Exhume hurt both players about equally and read level).

**Made-up monster powers: the trigger orders the cost** [project history · 6 to 9 November]

The 24 made-up elite powers combine 4 triggers with 7 effects (Strength, Block, Damage, PlainDamage, Weak, Vulnerable, Frail), minus the 4 pairs that real monsters already have. On average, the time a power fires orders its cost more than what it does. Powers that fire at the monster's own turn start or end cost the net 0.39 and 0.53 HP per elite fight, measured beyond a stat-only fight of matching difficulty. Powers that punish the player's own actions cost 1.62 (playing a card) and 2.98 (hitting the monster). The effect still matters: Weak costs over 2 HP on every trigger. Three mechanics are left out because the hand-written player collapsed on them. Against 'Vulnerable to the player on every card played' it won only 69% of elite fights, while the net won 85%.

- Setting: Generated-enemies bench: official Act 1 elites (Gremlin Nob, Lagavulin, three Sentries), each carrying one generated power. Cost = (29 October net @hpb0.01@tsl1, one turn deep, minus the hand-written player) on the mechanic, minus the same gap on the stat-only control nearest in difficulty, paired by start. · n: 675 elite starts x 16 seeds per mechanic; 21 mechanics (7 + 5 + 4 + 5) of the 24
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Each bar is a mean over 4 to 7 mechanics, and much of a mechanic's cost depends on which elite carries it. Nob alone drives the attack-trigger mean: 'damage when hit' costs +12.8 on Nob and under 2 on the others. The three left-out mechanics all sit on the player-action triggers and all read negative, because the hand-written player collapsed there, not because the net did well. Including them would lower those two bars. Turn-start and turn-end versions of one effect read almost alike, so there are about 19 distinct mechanics, not 24. The page's 1.28 HP headline is the mean of these 21.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | HP lost per elite fight beyond the hand-written player and the control (mean over mechanics) | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Monster's turn start | 0.39 | 7 mechanics; range -1.76 to +2.21 (Weak) |
| Monster's turn end | 0.53 | 5 mechanics; range -0.46 to +2.55 (Weak) |
| Player plays a card | 1.62 | 4 mechanics (Weak and Vulnerable left out; with them -1.00) |
| Player's attack takes the monster's HP | 2.98 | 5 mechanics (Vulnerable left out; with it +1.39) |

### Period 7 (P7): New nets for new content; relics and potions (10 to 12 November)

**Luck is a fifth of the noise, and subtracting it didn't help** [project history · 10 to 12 November]

On 11-11 the lab measured how much of a training label is luck. Replaying 1,200 positions 8 times each showed that luck after the decision is 19% of the labels' spread, not most of it. Subtracting the net's estimate of that luck removed only 13% of it (1% on Demon Form decks). On paired comparisons of two players over 100 card sequences, the luck-corrected difference was more spread out than the raw one (standard deviation 12.0 HP against 8.5). It needed a ratio of 0.75 or lower and got 1.41.

- Setting: 3,200 one-turn fights (1,600 elite, 1,600 boss) of the g1600 recipe, played by the 10-29 net @hpb0.01 on held-out decks; luck valued by the 10-29 net. Paired read: the same net with and without one setting, 100 sequences. · n: 22,813 labelled rows; 1,200 rows replayed 8 times; 100 paired sequences
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: One net values the luck. Its own error (0.147) is about twice the label noise, so a better net might price more of it. The 19% pools elite and boss fights: luck is 37% of the elite label spread and 20% of the boss spread. The ratio's se is wide (0.50): 1.41 is clearly above the 0.75 bar, but not clearly above 1.0, so 'no help' is firmer than 'worse'.

Chart (bignum) data:

| Item | value | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Luck's share of label spread | 19 | % (noise variance 0.071 of 0.364) |
| Luck removed by the correction | 13 | % (se 5); 1% on Demon Form decks |
| Paired-read spread, corrected / raw | 1.41 | ratio (se 0.50); bar was 0.75 or lower |

**Four made-up powers on one elite at once** [project history · 10 to 12 November]

Around midnight of 10-11 November the lab stacked four made-up monster powers on a single official elite, on 40 different random combinations. The 29 October net and a net trained from scratch on the real Act 1 pack both lost about 6 HP per fight more than the hand-written player, beyond what the plain elite costs. The net trained on a pack of made-up mechanics lost about half as much, 3.16 HP, on combinations it had never met together.

- Setting: Random-world bench, no-death mode: 40 worlds of 4 generated mechanics drawn from the 21-mechanic main pool, on 50 shared official elite starts x 4 seeds; each net @tsl1 one turn deep, cost = net's HP lost beyond the plain elite minus the hand-written player's, a death counted as the whole start HP · n: 40 worlds x 50 starts x 4 seeds per net (8,000 fights per player per net)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: One seed per new net. The generated-pack net trained on these mechanics singly, so the test is new combinations, not new mechanics. The 29 October net's worlds were played on 9 November (same worlds and starts); the two new nets' runs ran 10 Nov 23:41 to 11 Nov 00:14. With the start-HP cap, deaths dominate the remaining cost: the generated-pack net dies about as often as the hand-written player (0.49 vs 0.51 of games).

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | HP lost per elite fight beyond the hand-written player | Note |
|---|---|---|
| 29 October net | 6.38 | se 0.20 |
| Net trained from scratch on the real Act 1 pack (seed 1) | 6.16 | se 0.23 |
| Net trained on the made-up mechanics pack (seed 1) | 3.16 | se 0.18; paired cut -3.01 (0.18) |

### Period 8 (P8): The product changes; the real game (13 to 14 November)

#### Behind the scenes

**636 stored facts, 48 now suspect** [project history · 13 to 14 November]

By 11-14 the project's fact store (docs/claims) held 636 facts: 426 active, 210 retired. Measurements are the bulk (246 active). Lane facts are almost all retired (161 of 169) because a lane's facts retire when it closes. The real-game day left 48 active facts suspect: the store flags a fact automatically when a file it cites changes. 13 of the 34 'how' facts (how the code works) went suspect, because the day's rule fixes touched engine files (state.rs, monsters.rs, registry.rs) and scripts/gauntlet.py. Every file those facts cite was last changed on 11-14. The most-rewritten fact is lane/act1-content/step, revised 59 times as the Act 1 content work moved block by block.

- Setting: Every JSON fact under docs/claims (recursive) at the working tree of 11-14; suspect = listed by 'kb.py check'; one bar per area, value = active facts. · n: 636 facts (426 active, 210 retired, 48 suspect)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Suspect is a snapshot taken on the last day, right after many rule fixes, so it is higher than on a quiet day. A suspect fact may still be true; it only needs a re-check. The store started late on 11-02 (80 seeded facts), so facts were created in periods 4-8 only (85, 133, 201, 86, 131).

#### What it thinks hardest about

**Mostly instant, now and then a long think** [real game · A0 and A20, shown apart]

At Ascension 20 the AI makes a typical move in 0.15 seconds, and three moves in four take under a second. The slowest 10% of moves, though, used 73% of all its thinking time, and one move in a hundred took more than 28 seconds.

- Setting: Real game, A20, all run-rule versions pooled, runs 28-90 (63 runs, 3,579 fight decisions); A0 shown separately, runs 1-27 (27 runs, 1,739 fight decisions) · n: 3,579 A20 decisions; 1,739 A0 decisions
- Check: confirmed by an independent check
- How it was counted: These are wall-clock seconds on a laptop that other jobs shared, so single times are noisy. A0 and A20 used different net and search versions and different decks, so the two settings aren't a like-for-like comparison. In total the AI thought for about 2.4 hours in the A20 runs and 1.7 hours in the A0 runs. Percentiles take the sorted value at position p*N (linear interpolation gives values up to 0.1 s lower). The slowest-10% share is 72.5%, rounded to 73%.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | seconds per decision |
|---|---|
| A20 median | 0.15 |
| A20 90th percentile | 7.63 |
| A20 99th percentile | 28.29 |
| A0 median | 0.31 |
| A0 90th percentile | 12.37 |
| A0 99th percentile | 28.43 |

**Slime Boss is the fight it thinks hardest about** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

In a typical Slime Boss fight the AI thought for 81 seconds in total. In a typical Hexaghost fight it thought for 2 seconds, and against Gremlin Nob for about 1. Four of the five A20 fights with the most total thinking time were Slime Boss fights, and one of them took 7 minutes 20 seconds.

- Setting: Real game, A20, all run-rule versions pooled, runs 28-90; median total thinking seconds per fight for selected encounters, each seen at least 14 times (Gremlin Gang pooled over all its gremlin mixes) · n: 582 A20 fights; per row: Slime Boss 20, Gremlin Gang 20, Large Acid Slime 14, Sentries 15, The Guardian 14, Hexaghost 17, Lagavulin 18, Gremlin Nob 21, Jaw Worm 49, Cultist 52
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: These are totals for each fight, so longer fights add up more time. The median number of decisions was 12.5 for Slime Boss, 10 for Hexaghost and 13.5 for The Guardian, so fight length alone doesn't explain the gap. The slimes that split off also count toward the Slime Boss fight. The rows are a selection of encounters, not all of them. The fifth fight in the top five was Jaw Worm + Spike Slime (M) at 434 s.

**Every extra monster slows it down** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

With one monster left, the AI averages 1.22 seconds a move and only 4% of moves hit the search's node cap. With four on the board (gremlin gangs, slime splits) it averages 7.57 seconds, and 39% of those moves hit the cap.

- Setting: Real game, A20, all run-rule versions pooled, runs 28-90; fight decisions grouped by the number of living monsters that have not escaped · n: 3,579 A20 decisions (1 alive: 2,323; 2: 883; 3: 280; 4: 72; 5: 21 decisions over 10 runs, all in the Lots of Slimes fight)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: There are only 21 decisions with five monsters, and all of them come from one fight, the Lots of Slimes hallway. Each extra monster adds a target for every attack card: the average number of options goes from 4.2 with one monster to 14.7 with five, so the search has more to weigh. These numbers show that the two go together, not that the monster count alone causes the slowdown. The crowded fights also differ in other ways, such as the monsters' types and the turn number.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | mean seconds per decision | Note |
|---|---|---|
| 1 monster | 1.22 | n=2,323; median 0.08 s; 4% hit the cap |
| 2 monsters | 3.75 | n=883; median 0.45 s; 12% hit the cap |
| 3 monsters | 5.77 | n=280; median 3.15 s; 29% hit the cap |
| 4 monsters | 7.57 | n=72 (gremlin gangs, slime splits); median 6.0 s; 39% hit the cap |

**When the search runs out of room** [real game · A0 and A20, shown apart]

The search stops when it reaches a fixed number of positions (its node cap). At A20 that happened on 8.7% of moves (13.9% at A0). It hit the cap on 43% of the moves where it drank a potion, against 5% of the moves that played a Strike.

- Setting: Real game, A20, all run-rule versions pooled, runs 28-90; share of fight decisions where the search hit its node cap (cut = true). A0 runs 1-27 shown separately for comparison. · n: 3,579 A20 decisions, 313 hit the cap; A0 runs 1-27: 241 of 1,739
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Evolve and Juggernaut each come from only 4 runs, and one run (89, which had both) gives about half of their decisions and most of their capped ones. Without it both rates are about 39%, and one Juggernaut run never hit the cap. A potion adds its own targets to the options, and the AI tends to drink one in hard spots, so the 43% is about the spot as much as the potion. These are associations, not causes, and a capped move isn't necessarily a worse move.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | % of decisions that hit the node cap | Note |
|---|---|---|
| All A20 decisions | 8.7 | 313 of 3,579 |
| All A0 decisions | 13.9 | 241 of 1,739, A0 runs 1-27 |
| Move was a potion | 43 | 91 decisions, 51 runs; mean 9.7 s |
| Move was Strike | 5 | about 1,730 decisions |

**The single hardest moves** [real game · A0 and A20, shown apart]

Three of the AI's five slowest A20 moves came against Slime Boss on turn 3, though two of them were back-to-back moves in the same turn of one fight. In that turn it held Ascender's Bane, Bash+, a Strike and two Defends and thought for 96 seconds before playing Strike. Its slowest move overall was at A0, against three Red Louses, two still with Curl Up: it thought for 111 seconds and then played a single Strike.

- Setting: Real game, single fight decisions (card rewards excluded); A20 runs 28-90 (all run-rule versions pooled) and A0 runs 1-27 listed separately · n: the 6 slowest of 3,579 A20 decisions and the slowest of 1,739 A0 decisions
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: These are single moves on a shared laptop, so other jobs running at the same time may have added seconds. Two of the five A20 moves come from one turn of one fight. The rows show what was on the board, not why the move took long.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | seconds for one decision | Note |
|---|---|---|
| A20 run 44, Slime Boss 88 HP, turn 3 | 96.14 | hand Bash+, Strike, 2 Defend, Ascender's Bane; hit the cap; played Strike |
| A20 run 57, Acid Slime (L) + Spike Slime (M) | 91.24 | hand 2 Slimed, Anger, 2 Strike; Strength 3; Pen Nib + Shuriken; played Strike |
| A20 run 70, Spike Slime (L) + Acid Slime (L) | 78.79 | hand 2 Slimed, Anger+, Reckless Charge; Juggernaut 5, Feel No Pain 7; played Slimed |
| A20 run 44, Slime Boss 79 HP, same turn | 76.57 | next move of the same turn: Bash+, then a Fire Potion |
| A20 run 89, Slime Boss 120 HP, turn 3 | 66.78 | Juggernaut 5, Evolve in hand; drank a Swift Potion |
| A20 run 69, 2 Green Louses + Red Louse | 64.19 | Curl Up 12 on two louses; Reckless Charge in hand |
| A0 run 2, 3 Red Louses | 110.66 | Curl Up 5 and 6; Armaments in hand; hit the cap; played Strike |

#### What it picks in the real game

**A20: the cards it grabs when offered** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

At Ascension 20 the AI takes Juggernaut (6 of 8 offers), Impervious (6 of 9) and Rampage (6 of 10) more often than not. Its most-taken card overall is the common Armaments, picked 25 times out of 46 offers, more than any other card.

- Setting: Real game, A20, runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled (run-level stat: settings mixed). Cards offered at least 5 times. An offer counts only when the engine could load the card and so the AI could pick it (options list). · n: 554 card rewards (100 skipped), 63 runs; per-card offer counts in notes
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Most cards at the top have only 5-22 offers, so one or two picks move them a lot. Armaments (46 offers) is the only top card with a large sample. Many other cards were offered 40-60 times but are taken less often (Warcry 6 of 60, Headbutt 6 of 50, Flex 9 of 51). A card's pick rate also depends on what it was offered next to and on the deck at the time. Run-rule versions 1-5 are pooled.

Offers count only the times the card could actually be chosen.

**How often it skips the whole card reward** [real game · A0 and A20, shown apart]

About one card reward in five, the AI takes nothing: it rates 'skip' above every card offered. At Ascension 0 it got pickier late in the act (about a third skipped on floors 13-17); at Ascension 20 the skip rate barely moves with the floor.

- Setting: Real game, card-reward screens. A0: runs 1-27 (run rules 1). A20: runs 28-90, all run-rule versions and fight-guard settings pooled (picks are run-level, so treat the pooled A20 row as a mix of settings). · n: A0: 319 rewards in 27 runs; A20: 554 rewards in 63 runs
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The AI always took the option it scored highest (873 of 873 rewards), so a skip means the net scored the deck without the card higher. A0 floors 18+ come from the 16 of 27 A0 runs that reached Act 2, with only 45 rewards in all; no A20 run got past floor 17. By setting within A20, the skip rate ranges from 10% to 33%, but the groups with the extreme rates are tiny (10-21 rewards each). The two large groups, rules 2 (148 rewards) and rules 5 with the fight guard (298 rewards), sit at 16% and 18%, so treat the spread as noise.

**It doesn't chase rares** [real game · A0 and A20, shown apart]

Rare cards don't excite it: at Ascension 20 it took 29% of the rares it could pick, against 27% of commons and 27% of uncommons. At Ascension 0, uncommons were its favourite rarity (31%), and rares were no more popular than commons (26% against 25%).

- Setting: Real game, Ironclad card rewards from floor 1 on (Neow's floor-0 colorless choice left out). Counts only the cards the bot could take: 28 offered cards the engine doesn't model, such as Battle Trance and Reaper, are left out. Rarity comes from the game's own card data in states.jsonl. A0: runs 1-27 (run rules 1). A20: runs 28-90, all run-rule versions and both fight-guard settings pooled. · n: A0: 929 pickable cards in 314 rewards; A20: 1,641 pickable cards in 548 rewards
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: It can take at most one card per reward, so these rates are capped by its skip rate (about 18-20%) and by the other cards in the same reward. Rare samples are small (91 and 152 cards), and a few points' difference is within noise. The A0 uncommon lead is about 2 standard errors. Cards the engine doesn't model were never pickable and are left out. Counting them as offered would put A0 rares at 24% (24 of 100) and A0 uncommons at 30%. The A20 figures pool run-rule versions 1-5.

**A20: the deck at the Act 1 boss, boss by boss** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

Which boss a run met matters more than the cards. Hexaghost was beaten 4 times in 17 fights; Slime Boss and The Guardian together were beaten 26 times in 34. Most of the raw gap between cards comes from which boss each deck met. Wild Strike decks won only 3 of 9 boss fights, but 5 of those 9 were Hexaghost fights, all lost. Against Slime Boss or The Guardian, Wild Strike decks won 3 of 4, close to the 23 of 30 for the other decks. Fire Breathing stands out against both kinds of boss. Fire Breathing decks beat Hexaghost 2 times in 3, against 2 in 14 for the other decks. They also won all 5 of their fights against Slime Boss or The Guardian. Clash (10 of 11), Spot Weakness (8 of 9) and Pommel Strike (11 of 13) decks also beat Slime Boss and The Guardian more often than decks without those cards. Spot Weakness decks met Hexaghost only once, and Pommel Strike decks twice, so their good overall records partly reflect easier bosses.

- Setting: Real game, A20, runs 28-90. All run-rule versions and fight-guard settings are pooled. Only runs that reached the Act 1 boss (floor 16) are counted: 51 runs, one boss fight each. The deck is the run's deck during the boss fight, read from the floor-16 combat states, so a card from the boss's own reward isn't counted. Upgraded copies count as the base card. A card created during the fight, such as a random attack, doesn't count. 'Slime Boss/Guardian' pools the fights against Slime Boss (20) and The Guardian (14). · n: 51 A20 boss fights, 30 won: Hexaghost 17 (4 won), Slime Boss 20 (15 won), The Guardian 14 (11 won). Card groups are 3-13 fights.
- Check: confirmed by an independent check
- How it was counted: This shows correlation, not cause, and the groups are tiny. Most of these rows rest on 3-13 fights, and the Hexaghost rows rest on 3-5 fights. These cards were picked as the high and low ends among about 30 cards found in 5 or more boss decks, so a few of them land high or low by chance. Some gaps that look big in the pooled numbers vanish once the bosses are split. Armaments decks beat Slime Boss and The Guardian 11 times in 14, against 15 in 20 without it. Shrug It Off decks did so 9 times in 12, against 17 in 22. A plausible reason Fire Breathing helps against Hexaghost is that it deals damage whenever a Status card is drawn, and Hexaghost shuffles Burns into the deck. These records don't prove that link. The pooled settings changed the route through the map over time (rules 1-5, with and without the fight guard), so the decks and HP that reached the boss differ between settings too. The deck reading from the combat piles gives the same 9 Wild Strike decks as the master deck. A count of 10 appears only if a Wild Strike created during the fight is counted.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | % of A20 boss fights won, by boss and by a card in the deck at the boss | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Slime Boss/Guardian, any deck | 76 | 26 of 34 |
| Hexaghost, any deck | 24 | 4 of 17 |
| Fire Breathing vs Hexaghost | 67 | 2 of 3 (other decks 2 of 14) |
| Fire Breathing vs Slime Boss/Guardian | 100 | 5 of 5 (other decks 21 of 29) |
| Clash vs Slime Boss/Guardian | 91 | 10 of 11 (other decks 16 of 23); vs Hexaghost 2 of 5 |
| Spot Weakness vs Slime Boss/Guardian | 89 | 8 of 9 (other decks 18 of 25); met Hexaghost once and lost |
| Pommel Strike vs Slime Boss/Guardian | 85 | 11 of 13 (other decks 15 of 21); vs Hexaghost 0 of 2 |
| Wild Strike vs Slime Boss/Guardian | 75 | 3 of 4 (other decks 23 of 30): no real gap |
| Wild Strike vs Hexaghost | 0 | 0 of 5 (other decks 4 of 12) |

#### What hurts

**What ended the A20 runs** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

Of the 63 Ascension 20 runs, 33 died in Act 1. Hexaghost alone ended 13 of them: 13 of the 17 runs that reached it. Lagavulin was the deadliest elite: it ended 6 of the 17 runs that fought it (18 fights), against 2 deaths in 21 Gremlin Nob fights.

- Setting: Real game, A20 runs 28-90 (run rules 1-5, fight guard 0 and 1 pooled: rules 1-4 and rules 5 without guard = 30 runs, rules 5 with guard = 33 runs). Counted per run: the fight the run died in. · n: 63 runs, 33 deaths
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: This is run-level data pooled across all five run-rule versions and both fight-guard settings. The path rules change which fights a run takes and how much HP it brings to them. The three hallway deaths came from runs that started those fights with 10-12 HP, not from strong monsters. The counts per monster are small (one to 21 fights each), so the elite comparison is a hint, not a measured rate.

| Gremlin Nob | 2 | met 21 times |

**HP lost per encounter at A20** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

In the A20 hallways, the Gremlin Gang cost about 18 HP a fight on average, twice a lone Jaw Worm's 8.8, though a few bad fights pull that average up (median 12). A Cultist cost only 3.4 HP: those fights end fast, before Ritual's Strength has stacked much.

- Setting: Real game, A20 runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled. HP lost = HP at fight start minus HP at fight end, before end-of-fight heals such as Burning Blood (heals during the fight count, so a few fights show a gain). Every encounter met at least 14 times; mixed groups of lice, gremlins and sentries are counted as one encounter. · n: 582 A20 fights in 63 runs. The n for each encounter is in its note.
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: A fight that ended in death counts only the HP that was left, so averages for deadly fights are capped by the HP the bot brought in. 13 of the 17 Hexaghost fights and 6 of the 18 Lagavulin fights were deaths. Decks differ from fight to fight. Pooling rule versions is fine for how a fight goes, but the mix of decks differs between versions (for example, Hexaghost averaged 49.5 under rules 1 and about 63 later). Heals during a fight (potions, relics) count, so a few hallway fights show a small gain.

#### How it plays

**Strike and Defend are six in ten of all cards played** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

At Ascension 20 the bot played 7,792 cards, and 36% of them were plain Strikes and 25% plain Defends. The first card it picked up from a reward to make the top five is Anger. At Ascension 0 the picture is almost the same: Strike 35%, Defend 23%, then Anger at 5%.

- Setting: Real game, A20 (runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled), 582 fights, 2,922 turns. A0 (runs 1-27) is reported separately in the claim: 3,663 card plays. · n: 7,792 card plays at A20; 3,663 at A0
- Check: confirmed by an independent check
- How it was counted: The record holds re-plans, not single clicks. Plays are rebuilt from each decision's planned line, cut where the next re-plan of the same turn starts. The last plan of a fight can list cards after the killing blow, so the counts can be a little high. Fights the fallback rule finished count only up to the hand-over. Upgraded cards are counted separately (Bash and Bash+ together would be 6.5% at A20 and 5.5% at A0, ahead of Anger).

**Whirlwind always gets played; Evolve rarely does** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

When a card sits in the opening hand of a turn, how often does the bot play it that turn? Whirlwind gets played 92% of the time and Flex 88%. Evolve is played only 21% of the time, Fire Breathing about a third of the time, and even Bash only 44%. Strike (74%) gets played far more often than Defend (57%). At A0 the same pattern holds: Evolve 14%, Demon Form and Fire Breathing 26%.

- Setting: Real game, A20 (runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled), 2,922 turns. Only cards in the turn's opening hand at least 20 times are shown. · n: 2,922 turns; each row gives played/held
- Check: confirmed by an independent check
- How it was counted: A turn counts as played if the card was played at least once. Copies drawn mid-turn are not counted. A power that was played leaves the deck, so its later turns never count against it. Plays are rebuilt from planned lines (see plays-most-played). Upgraded copies are separate rows (Bash+ is played 55% of the time).

**Bash on turn 1? It depends who is across the table** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

With Bash in the opening hand at A20, the bot played it on turn 1 in 113 of 207 fights and played it as its first card 70 times. It Bashed every Cultist (18 of 18), every Gremlin Nob (7 of 7) and every Slime Boss (6 of 6). It mostly held Bash against lice (2 of 11 against a Green and Red Louse pair, 0 of 6 against a lone Red Louse), against Lagavulin (1 of 7) and against an Acid Slime (M) with a Spike Slime (S) (2 of 10). Lice curl up and Lagavulin starts asleep, which may be part of why, but the data doesn't show the reason.

- Setting: Real game, A20 (runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled): fights where Bash or Bash+ was in the turn-1 hand. Rows are all monster groups with at least 6 such fights (16 groups). A group is the set of monster names in the first turn-1 view. · n: 207 fights with Bash in the opening hand (A20, 62 runs)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Many groups have small counts (6 to 22 fights), so each row shows what happened in these runs, not a fixed rule. Holding Bash on turn 1 doesn't mean it was never played later. "First card" skips potions: counting a potion used first as the lead gives 66 instead of 70. At A0 (runs 1-27), Bash was played on turn 1 in 59 of 94 fights and was the first card in 33.

**It rarely Defends against Gremlin Nob** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

Gremlin Nob's Enrage gives it Strength every time you play a Skill. The bot plays its Defends sparingly there: in 74 Nob turns with a Defend in hand it played one on only 17 (23%), against 154 of 216 (71%) at the other elites (Lagavulin and Sentries). Across all Nob turns it played 23 Defends to 101 Strikes, the lowest Defend-to-Strike ratio of any enemy group. It won 19 of its 21 Nob fights. The other end of the scale is Acid Slime (L) and The Guardian, where it blocked more than it struck.

- Setting: Real game, A20 (runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled). Rows are every single-enemy group (one monster name in view) with at least 40 Strike+Defend plays. Multi-enemy groups are left out of the rows; the "lowest of any enemy group" statement was checked against all 29 qualifying groups, mixed ones included. The Gremlin Gang (1.20) is left out because a single 84-turn fight dominates it (one 84-turn fight, run 83). · n: 21 Gremlin Nob fights (90 turns); 2,922 turns in all
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: Defend and Defend+ are counted together, and so are Strike and Strike+. A group is all monsters in view on that turn. The decision log has no line per card played. The plays were rebuilt from each search's planned line, up to where the next search of the same turn starts. Whether the bot held its Skills because of Enrage or for another reason (Nob fights are short races) can't be read from these numbers. At A0 there were only 3 Nob fights (2 Defends played in 8 turns with one in hand).

**Potions go down early, often on turn 1** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

At A20 the bot drank 242 potions, and 176 of them (73%) on turn 1 of a fight. Its HP at the time was usually high: the median was 82% of max HP, and only 17 potions were drunk below half HP. It drank more potions per fight at bosses (25 in 51 fights) and elites (31 in 54) than in hallway fights (186 in 477). Energy, Weak and Fear Potions top the list. Fairy in a Bottle, which works on its own, sat in the belt for 120 turns.

- Setting: Real game, A20 (runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled), 582 fights. At A0 (runs 1-27, 332 fights), 109 potions were drunk, 78 of them on turn 1, at a median of 96% HP. · n: 242 potion uses at A20 (each a planned Potion action confirmed by that potion leaving the belt)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The potion's name is read from the slot in the bot's view of its belt. A use counts when a Potion action in a planned line matches a potion that is gone by the bot's next decision; 8 planned uses with no matching belt loss are left out. Turn of use at A20: 176 on turn 1, 50 on turn 2, 11 on turn 3, 5 later. A turn-1 potion isn't necessarily a waste: Weak, Fear and setup potions do the most when used early. The boss and elite numbers are small (51 and 54 fights), and the per-fight difference is an association: it doesn't show that the bot saves potions for those fights.

**Never dies with a potion** [real game · A0 and A20, shown apart]

Whenever the engine played a losing fight to the end, its potion belt was empty when the run died: 33 of 33 deaths at Ascension 20 and 2 of 2 at Ascension 0 in Act 1. The third A0 Act 1 death (run 16, a boss fight handed to the fallback rule partway through) also had an empty belt. The Act 2 fights at A0 were played by a fallback rule that never drinks potions, and 13 of those 16 deaths still had potions unused.

- Setting: Real game. Potions held at the run's last state (the GAME_OVER screen for deaths, the Act 2 map for stops). Rows: A20 deaths (all run-rule versions, runs 28-90); A0 Act 1 deaths (runs 8, 16, 26; run 16's boss fight was finished by the fallback rule); A0 Act 2 deaths (runs 1-20, fallback rule); A20 runs that beat the boss and stopped at Act 2 (all run-rule versions). Runs 3, 5 and 21 (left early or restarted) and the A0 boss-beating stops (runs 22-27) are not in any row. · n: 33 + 3 + 16 deaths, and 30 boss-beating A20 runs
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: This shows the engine had used up its potions before dying, not that drinking them earlier would have saved the run. The A0 Act 1 row has only 3 deaths, and one of them (run 16) was finished by the fallback rule. A20 pools run-rule versions 1-5 and fight guard 0/1.

**Ending the turn with energy and a playable card** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

One 84-turn gremlin standoff (run 83, floor 5) is set aside here because it alone gave a quarter of the cases. Without it, the bot at A20 ended 8.9% of its turns with energy left over, and 5.3% with both energy and a card it could afford to play. The leftover card was usually a Strike. Without that fight Havoc almost never shows up (1 case). The rate depends a lot on the enemy. With The Guardian or a lone Acid Slime (L) on the board it was about 17%. With Jaw Worm, Cultist or Hexaghost it was close to 0. A0 is almost the same once its one long stall (run 18, floor 2, a lone Acid Slime (S)) is set aside: 8.8% and 5.2%. With that fight counted, both levels sit at 10.3% energy left and 6.8-6.9% energy plus an affordable card.

- Setting: Real game, A20 (runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled). Turn ends come from the game's own states: the last state of a turn before the next turn starts on the same floor. Fights the fallback rule finished are left out, and so is the run 83 floor 5 fight against Mad Gremlin + Shield Gremlin. Each row is the group of monsters still standing at that turn end. The A0 rates (runs 1-27, with and without the run 18 floor 2 fight) are given only in the text, never on the same chart. · n: A20: 2,254 turn ends in 568 fights, with 200 ending with energy left and 120 with energy and an affordable card. Counting run 83 floor 5 gives 2,337 turn ends, 241 and 161. A0: 982 turn ends in 265 fights, with 86 and 51. Counting run 18 floor 2 gives 1,009, 104 and 69.
- Check: confirmed by an independent check
- How it was counted: Affordable means the game marked the card as playable and its cost was no more than the energy left. Status cards such as Slimed count (10 cases at A20). The last turn of each fight isn't counted. Holding a card can be the right play, for example to avoid triggering The Guardian's Mode Shift or to keep an Acid Slime (L) above its split threshold. These numbers don't say whether it was, and an enemy's high rate doesn't show that the enemy caused it. Most rows have only 5-27 cases, and the Slaver rate comes mostly from one fight. The A0 rate with run 18 counted is 6.8% or 6.9%, depending on how turn pairs split by a non-combat state are handled.

#### Records

**The longest fights: an 84-turn gremlin standoff** [real game · A0 and A20, shown apart]

The longest real-game fight lasted 84 turns. In A20 run 83 (floor 5), a Shield Gremlin with 4 HP shielded the remaining Mad Gremlin, giving it 11 Block every turn, for about 69 turns (turns 14 to 82). On 67 of those turns the net struck the shielded Mad Gremlin rather than the 4-HP Shield Gremlin. The two strikes scored almost the same (1.51 vs 1.49). Its HP drained from 35 to 15 before it finally killed the Shield Gremlin, and it won on 8 HP. The A0 record has a different cause. In run 18, floor 2, a Small Acid Slime sat at 4 HP for about 23 turns while the net played Defend, Defend, End. Every option scored exactly the same (2.01), so to the search killing the slime looked no better than waiting.

- Setting: Real game, Act 1 fights (floors 1-16). A20: runs 28-90, run-rule and fight-guard versions pooled (fight-level stat), 582 fights. A0: runs 1-27, 272 Act 1 fights. Turns come from the game's turn counter in states.jsonl. · n: A20 582 fights, A0 272 fights; the median fight lasts 4 turns at both ascensions
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: These are single records, not trends. The two long stalls come from the search seeing no difference between options (A0: every option scored 2.01) or almost none (A20 run 83: 1.51 vs 1.49), not from the monsters. A0 and A20 rows are listed separately and never averaged together. Rows tied at 15 turns are all shown.

**Meat on the Bone's big nights** [real game · A0 and A20, shown apart]

Burning Blood does steady work: +6 HP after nearly every win (507 of 549 A20 wins), 3,207 HP in all. A20 run 77, the only A20 run that held Meat on the Bone, got that +18 in four of its five wins after picking it up, the last after The Guardian (23 to 41 HP). At A0, Black Blood (+12) shows up only in Act 2.

Meat on the Bone: A20 5 fights in 1 run (4 heals above 6); A0 13 fights in 2 runs (4 heals above 6). Black Blood: A0 4 fights in 2 runs (runs 13, 14), floors 18-21.
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The heal is capped by max HP, so a fight that ends near full health shows a smaller heal. Relics are read from the game state at the fight's last turn. A few won fights (A20: 4, A0: 2) show a small negative heal because of recording glitches; they are kept in the means.

#### What the net believes

**When the net says 'won', it wins** [real game · A0 and A20, shown apart]

At Ascension 20, the net scored 464 fights at 1.80 or higher at the start, and it won every one of them but one, a Lagavulin. Below 1.0 it expected to lose, and 21 of those 24 fights were lost. In the fights it won, it was a pessimist about HP: it expected to lose about 11 HP and actually lost about 7.6 (after Burning Blood's heal, which its score counts too).

- Setting: Real game, Act 1 fights that the net played to the end. A20: runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled, 579 fights. A0: runs 1-27, 266 fights. Fights handed to the fallback rule are left out (3 at A20, 6 at A0), and so is the one Act 2 fight the net played at A0. The score is the chosen first action's score at the fight's first decision on turn 1: the two-turn score, or the one-turn score where the record has no two-turn score (82 fights at A20, 68 at A0). · n: A20 579 fights (33 lost); A0 266 fights (2 lost)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: The score mixes the chance of winning with the expected HP loss. A win scores 2 minus HP lost/100, where HP lost is counted after Burning Blood's heal and floored at 0. A loss scores 0. So a score of 1.6 can mean a near-certain win that costs 40 HP, or a likely win with some risk, and the buckets are not pure probabilities. Some fights' first decisions have no two-turn score, so the one-turn score is used for them. The 1.0-1.7 buckets at A20 have only 19-37 fights each, and A0 has only 2 losses. Before the heal, won A20 fights cost about 12 HP each.

|---|---|---|

| A20, 1.5-1.7 | 83.8 | 31 of 37 won |
| A20, 1.7-1.8 | 100 | 35 of 35 won |
| A20, 1.8-1.9 | 99.2 | 118 of 119 won (the loss: run 79, Lagavulin) |
| A20, 1.9 and up | 100 | 345 of 345 won |
| A0, start score < 1.5 | 77.8 | 7 of 9 won |
| A0, 1.5-1.8 | 100 | 24 of 24 won |

At A20 the net's fight-start score was furthest above the real result against Hexaghost (13 of 17 fights lost) and Lagavulin (6 of 18 lost). Against The Guardian it was the other way round: in the 11 fights it won, its start score was worth about 70 HP lost, and it actually lost about 34 HP.

The 12 other encounters with at least 6 fights, such as Looter, Louse and Blue Slaver, all lie between -0.4 and -4.8. · n: 282 fights across the shown encounters (524 across all 23 encounters with at least 6 fights; 579 fights at A20 in all)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: A loss counts as score 0, so one loss moves an encounter's mean a lot. The boss and elite rows rest on 14-21 fights each. The score mixes the chance of winning with the HP lost, so "worth about 70 HP" is the score expressed in HP, not a pure HP forecast. At A0 (runs 1-27) the pattern is different: it was too gloomy about The Guardian (n=13), Slime Boss (n=7), Hexaghost (only 3 fights) and Lagavulin (8 fights, none lost). Only the Sentries came out slightly too hopeful (+4.0, n=12).

**When looking one more turn ahead changed the verdict** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

In run 34 (A20), the Red Slaver fight on floor 14 started at 9 HP. One turn deep, the best option scored 0.15, which reads as an almost certain loss. Two turns deep, every attacking line scored about 1.52. The search played the Skill Potion, and the fight was won: 4 HP at the end, 10 after the end-of-fight heal. The reverse also happens: in run 78, against a Looter at 11 HP, one turn deep looked like a comfortable win at 1.58. Two turns deep it scored 0.43, and that fight was lost.

- Setting: Real game, A20, runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled. 3,175 decisions with at least two options where the two-turn search finished (cut decisions have no two-turn score). Value = two-turn score of the chosen option minus the best one-turn score. Each fight is shown once, at its most extreme decision. · n: 3,175 decisions; the 4 fights with the largest rises and the 4 with the largest drops are shown (one decision per fight)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: These are hand-picked extremes from 3,175 decisions, not typical ones, and each fight is shown once. The scores are the net's beliefs, not outcomes: run 69's two-turn hope against The Guardian did not hold. In run 34 the second turn raised every attacking line (Strike and Cleave also scored about 1.52), so the potion was not the only saving line. At A0 (runs 1-27) the biggest rise was run 17's Hexaghost fight, turn 3: Defend went from 0.23 to 1.26, and the fight was won.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | two-turn minus one-turn score (score points; 1.0 = 100 HP-equivalents) |
|---|---|
| Run 34 fl 14 Red Slaver, 9 HP: Skill Potion (won) | 1.365 |
| Run 69 fl 16 The Guardian, 44 HP: Bash+ (lost) | 0.918 |
| Run 78 fl 13 Looter, 11 HP: Thunderclap (lost) | -1.154 |
| Run 76 fl 8 Lagavulin, 40 HP, turn 10: Clothesline (lost) | -1.053 |
| Run 79 fl 10 Lagavulin, 32 HP, turn 9: Strike (lost) | -0.888 |
| Run 52 fl 10 Gremlin Nob, 44 HP: Defend (lost) | -0.887 |

**Looking two turns ahead: start with Strike, not Defend** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

About one decision in five, the two-turn search picked a first action that was not among the best-scoring ones one turn deep: 630 of 3,175 at A20 and 307 of 1,446 at A0. At A20 Strike was the two-turn pick in 351 of those 630 changes. Defend was a one-turn favourite overruled for Strike 138 times, and the reverse happened only 24 times.

- Setting: Real game, A20, runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled; 3,175 uncut decisions with at least two options. A0 is runs 1-27, 1,446 decisions. Rows are A20 changes counted from each card that tied for the best one-turn score to the two-turn pick. A decision with tied favourites counts once for each tied card, so the rows overlap. Changes that only switched a card's target are left out of the rows. · n: 630 changed picks at A20 (19.8%); 307 at A0 (21.2%)
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: An option here is the first action of a planned turn, so many changes only reorder the same turn. One-turn scores often tie: in 343 of the 630 A20 changes, two or more options shared the best one-turn score. The median one-turn cost of the overruled choice was small: 0.64 HP-equivalents at A20 and 0.40 at A0. Only 71 of the 630 A20 changes cost more than 5 HP-equivalents one turn deep. Changes where the fight guard picked the option are included.

Chart (bars) data:

| Item | decisions (A20) | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Defend -> Strike | 138 | Defend the sole one-turn favourite in 47 |
| Bash -> Strike | 37 |  |
| Strike -> Defend | 24 |  |
| Bash+ -> Strike | 17 |  |
| Shrug It Off -> Strike | 15 |  |
| Armaments -> Strike | 13 |  |
| Armaments -> Defend | 13 |  |
| End turn -> Strike | 11 |  |
| Strike -> Bash+ | 10 |  |

**Cards the net most wants to play rather than end the turn** [real game · A20 · rule versions pooled]

At A20, when Rampage or Ghostly Armor was playable, the net typically scored playing it about 22 points above ending the turn right there. Whirlwind came last of the cards shown, at about 10, from only 4 runs.

- Setting: Real game, A20, runs 28-90, all run-rule versions pooled. 3,266 uncut decisions where End was an option. Per card or potion: the best two-turn score among options that start by playing it (the same card aimed at several targets counts once), minus End's two-turn score in the same decision. Only cards and potions with at least 20 such decisions. · n: 57 cards and potions with n >= 20, each shown with its n (decisions); the number of runs behind each is in the notes
- Check: corrected by its independent check
- How it was counted: This measures each card in the hands where it showed up, not the card on its own. Ending a turn with unused energy is always costly, and a card held during desperate fights picks up that context. Many cards come from only a handful of runs (Havoc and Ghostly Armor from 5 each), so their values reflect a few decks. Turns close to lethal pull the averages far up, so medians are shown. At A0 (runs 1-27, 1,498 decisions) the top cards were Defend+ (+18.5, n=77) and Searing Blow (+17.9, n=20, 2 runs), and the bottom card was Demon Form (+3.5, n=28, 3 runs).

| Ghostly Armor | 22.3 | n=63, 5 runs |
| Perfected Strike+ | 21.6 | n=25, 5 runs |
| Flex | 19.7 | n=84, 12 runs |
| Shockwave | 19.1 | n=35, 6 runs |
| Spot Weakness | 17.9 | n=81, 12 runs |
| Speed Potion | 17.7 | n=22, 8 runs |
| Carnage | 17.5 | n=61, 8 runs |
| Fire Breathing | 12 | n=70, 10 runs |
| Intimidate | 11.2 | n=32, 7 runs |
| Whirlwind | 10.1 | n=36, 4 runs |

Everything tried

## Every experiment, change and tool, one line each

In order, stretch by stretch, whether it worked or not. Lanes (the AI roles' short research projects) say how likely they rated themselves before they ran. 

Outcome words: Worked, Didn't work, Partly, Built, Dropped, Still open. Lanes say how likely they rated themselves before they ran and the CPU-hours they used. 318 entries.

### Stretch 1: The engine and the first question (23 Oct to 24 Oct)

15 things tried: Worked 3, Didn't work 3, Partly 1, Built 7, Dropped 1.

#### Engine

- **Rust Act 1 combat engine.** They and Claude built a Rust combat engine for the Ironclad in Act 1 at Ascension 0, with the in-scope cards, the hallway monsters, the three elites and Slime Boss, and a draw pile the AI cannot peek into. (23 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Step 2: full content)
- **Score counts HP after healing.** A won fight was scored by the HP lost after Burning Blood's end-of-combat heal, so the AI is not punished for damage the relic gives back. (24 Oct; Built; source: commit 6115ceff)
- **Powers as triggered rules.** Powers like Enrage, Curl Up and Spore Cloud were rebuilt as rules that fire on triggers, Combust, Brutality and Rupture were added, and cards with conditions such as Spot Weakness and Dropkick came in. (24 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Triggers and conditions (SPEC.md Amendment 4))

#### Search

- **Afterstate search and heuristic.** The AI picks a play by comparing the positions each choice leads to, searching every way to play out the turn and scoring the end positions with a hand-written formula called the heuristic. (23 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Step 3: options, search, agents)
- **Deeper teacher sizing.** Heuristic search looking two turns ahead with four sampled next-turn draws picked better moves on the bench's contested decisions but did not win more tight fights, at about a hundred times the cost. (24 Oct; Partly; source: docs/research/learned-play-results.md#Step 1: teacher sizing (2026-10-24))

#### Training the net

- **Learned-play switches sweep.** An overnight sweep tried eight mixes of training tricks (search values as targets, a ranking loss, an HP-loss target) over self-play rounds, and no mix won more held-out tight fights than plain heuristic search. (24 Oct to 25 Oct; Didn't work; source: runs/sweep-2026-10-25.md)
- **Ground-truth targets, HP histogram.** Training switched to real fight outcomes as targets and added a head that predicts how much HP is still to be lost as a histogram, and the nets still stayed below the heuristic. (24 Oct to 25 Oct; Didn't work; source: docs/decisions.md#Learned play Amendment 1 as built (provisional))

#### Unseen content

- **Effects net vs names net.** Two small value nets learned to score positions from self-played fights, one reading cards as what they do and one reading only card names, and on games with five held-out cards the effects net's prediction error was half the names net's, passing check 6. (23 Oct to 24 Oct; Worked; source: commit 39de9ebf)
- **Silent transfer test.** Nets trained only on Ironclad fights were loaded unchanged on Silent decks, and on the Silent's tier-A decks the effects net's prediction error was 27% lower than the names net's, passing check 8. (24 Oct; Worked; source: commit 647225dd)
- **Check 9: unseen power cards.** The effects net was asked to value playing held-out power cards like Demon Form, Metallicize and Rage, and on those games its error was only 18.3% lower than the names net's against a 20% bar, with Demon Form understood least. (24 Oct; Didn't work; source: commit 0048487e)
- **Generated cards draft.** A draft for randomly generated cards to widen training was written and parked, and a narrower version for generated powers was built later as registry-lite. (24 Oct to 31 Oct; Dropped; source: docs/specs/generated-cards.md)

#### Tests and rulers

- **Evaluation bench, tight fights v1.** A test bench was built with fights started at the HP where heuristic search wins about half the time, plus decision sets with cached deeper answers, so small differences in play would show. (24 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Evaluation bench as built (2026-10-24))

#### Tools and process

- **Python bindings and card features.** The engine got a Python module that runs fights in bulk and turns a position into numbers a neural network can read, with cards described either by their effects or by their names. (23 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Step 4: Python bindings, features, replay)
- **Golden trajectories test.** A test replays 9,000 recorded random games move for move, so any engine rewrite that changes play is caught at once. (24 Oct; Built; source: commit ddf72add)
- **Denormal flush speed fix.** Rounding tiny floating-point numbers to zero made training and net-guided search about ten times faster on the laptop's CPU. (24 Oct; Worked; source: commit 78e0f18c)

### Stretch 2: Self-play, with the owner at the desk (25 Oct to 28 Oct)

14 things tried: Worked 2, Didn't work 3, Partly 1, Built 8.

#### Engine

- **Rules-engine umbrella design.** They and Claude designed a series of ten engine specs so new mechanics could later be added as data, with saved nets still reading positions exactly as before. (26 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Rules-engine umbrella review (2026-10-26))

#### Training the net

- **Branch rollouts, heuristic pool.** Self-play also played out side branches at close decisions and kept a large pool of heuristic games in training, and the Colab sweep of these four recipes still left every net under the heuristic on tight fights. (25 Oct; Didn't work; source: docs/decisions.md#Learned play Amendment 2 as built (provisional))
- **Varied setups and stop rule.** Training games got random start HP and random decks and runs stopped once a round beat the heuristic, but over ten rounds no round did and the rounds plateaued because the nets learned the value of their own noisy exploring play. (25 Oct to 26 Oct; Didn't work; source: docs/research/learned-play-results.md#Amendment 3 sweep and why rounds don't improve (2026-10-26))
- **Greedy self-play.** Self-play stopped exploring at random and played its best move, with side branches covering the alternatives, which became the recipe the curriculum night used. (26 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Learned play Amendment 4 as built (provisional))
- **Curriculum night.** Nets trained on exact answers near the end of fights and on search answers further out reached the heuristic's level on tight fights but never got past it, and the solver's trusted zone stayed one turn from the end. (26 Oct to 28 Oct; Didn't work; source: docs/research/learned-play-results.md#Curriculum night (2026-10-28))
- **Exact-only training.** Nets trained only on solver answers learned to pick well one turn from the end on database positions but not on the bench puzzles, and a rerun on more positions closed most of that gap. (28 Oct; Partly; source: docs/research/learned-play-results.md#Exact-only experiment, rerun on positions walked with bounds (2026-10-28))

#### Tests and rulers

- **Exact solver and puzzle bench.** An exact solver finds the provably best play near the end of a fight, stores solved positions in a database and turns real game endings into a puzzle bench. (26 Oct to 27 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Exact solver and position finder (provisional, spec docs/specs/exact-solver.md))
- **Paired deciding gate.** A net now passed only if its win lead over the heuristic, fight by fight on the same tight fights, stayed above zero on both the training-card and held-out-card halves. (28 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Learned play Amendment 5 as built (provisional, 2026-10-28))

#### Tools and process

- **Colab sweeps.** Sweeps could run one recipe per free Google Colab session, with results stored on Google Drive, while the dev machine was busy. (25 Oct; Built; source: commit 0c32da6d)
- **Net forward pass in Rust.** The value net's scoring was rewritten in Rust and built with LTO and mimalloc so search could call it directly, which re-sized every later sweep. (25 Oct; Worked; source: commit 7440ef82)
- **Sweep watcher.** A watcher process followed each overnight sweep's progress, memory and speed and woke the session when something crashed or stalled. (25 Oct; Built; source: commit 03ce437c)
- **Memory-mapped training caches.** Training data moved to files read straight from disk, so a night's training no longer had to fit in memory at once. (27 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Curriculum Amendment 1 as built (provisional, 2026-10-27))
- **Solved-position explorer.** A small web page let them browse solved positions and puzzles and step through each option the solver and the nets would pick. (27 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Explorer as built (provisional, 2026-10-27))
- **Solver bounds.** The solver learned to skip options that cannot beat the best one found so far, and on the night's two slowest rounds it found five to seven times as many positions. (28 Oct; Worked; source: docs/research/learned-play-results.md#Solver bounds, measured (2026-10-28))

### Stretch 3: Claude runs the lab day to day (29 Oct to 31 Oct)

46 things tried: Worked 12, Didn't work 19, Partly 4, Built 11.

#### Engine

- **Saved-net contract.** Every net is now saved with the names of the input columns it reads, so a net keeps working unchanged when new kinds of input are added later. (29 Oct to 30 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Contract as built (owner away, 2026-10-30))
- **Combat turn limit.** A fight still running after 100 turns now counts as a loss, after an untrained net blocked every hit forever and hung a test for four hours. (30 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Play turn limit (owner away, 2026-10-30))
- **Hexaghost and The Guardian.** The two missing Act 1 bosses were added with Divider, Inferno, Burn, Mode Shift and Sharp Hide, every number checked against the wiki. (30 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Act-1 bosses as built (owner away, 2026-10-30))
- **Registry-lite generated powers.** Generated power cards could now be loaded from a file at runtime, giving goal 1 a supply of new cards built from known pieces. (31 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Registry-lite spec confirmed (owner away, 2026-10-31))

#### Search

- **Shared random draws in search.** Two-turn search now gives every option the same sampled draws, so options are compared under the same luck instead of different luck. (30 Oct; Worked; source: docs/decisions.md#Search shares sampled outcomes (owner away, 2026-10-30))
- **Depth pilot.** In a 60-fight pilot on held-out elites and bosses, two-turn search won fewer fights than one-turn search, which exposed two bugs: options compared under different draws and a node cap that made cut-off options look good. (30 Oct; Didn't work; source: docs/research/depth-pilot-2026-10-30.md)
- **Larger node cap: depth stays.** With the node cap raised and shared draws, two-turn heuristic search beat one-turn by 0.056 of a win on train elite and boss fights of tight fights v2, so depth was kept. (30 Oct; Worked; source: docs/decisions.md#Depth stays; guarded player before the engine fix (owner away, 2026-10-30))
- **Fallback two-turn player.** Using one-turn search's choice wherever the two-turn tree was cut short lifted two-turn heuristic search further on held-out elites and bosses, and became the two-turn player. (31 Oct; Worked; source: docs/decisions.md#Two-turn player: fallback, no margin (owner away, 2026-10-31))
- **Margin guard for depth.** Letting two-turn search override one-turn only when it was sure by a margin cost wins, especially on Slime Boss, so the guard was dropped. (31 Oct; Didn't work; source: docs/decisions.md#Two-turn player: fallback, no margin (owner away, 2026-10-31))
- **Net leaves under depth.** Two-turn search with the champion net scoring the leaves beat one-turn net play on held-out elites and bosses, so depth and the net's gains add up. (31 Oct; Worked; source: docs/decisions.md#Depth with net leaves passes: distillation next (owner away, 2026-10-31))
- **Top-3 pruning on puzzles.** Limiting two-turn search to one-turn's three best options picked as well on endgame puzzles but saved almost no work, since those puzzles have few options. (31 Oct; Didn't work; source: docs/research/learned-play-results.md#Two first checks from the review (fork of manager run bb961751, 16:55 2026-10-31))
- **Top-3 two-turn player.** Choosing two-turn search's favourite among one-turn's top three options played the same as the plain fallback player on held-out elites and bosses, so the simpler player stayed. (31 Oct; Didn't work; source: docs/decisions.md#Top-3 two-turn player: no change (owner away, 2026-10-31))
- **Prior art on stochastic card games.** A literature scan on search in card games with random draws framed the product as net plus search at play time, with the net trained to be a better leaf. (31 Oct; Built; source: docs/decisions.md#Prior art: search in stochastic card games (owner away, 2026-10-31))
- **Ensemble of seed nets.** Scoring leaves with the average of three same-recipe nets helped a little one turn deep but almost nothing under two-turn search, because depth already fixes the same errors. (31 Oct to 1 Nov; Didn't work; source: docs/decisions.md#Ensemble evaluator: no change at two turns; spec folded (owner away, 2026-11-01))

#### Training the net

- **Curriculum Amendment 3 check.** Retraining the night's last round with more exact labels a turn or two from the end fit the end of fights a little better but did not move tight-fight wins at either label cap. (29 Oct; Didn't work; source: docs/research/learned-play-results.md#Curriculum Amendment 3, one-round check (2026-10-29))
- **Play-out average labels.** Labelling positions with the average of several games played on from them, instead of one game's ending, gained nothing once averaged over three training seeds. (29 Oct; Didn't work; source: docs/decisions.md#Rung 4 check as built (2026-10-29))
- **First champion: the 10-29 net.** A net retrained during the seed check became the first to beat the heuristic on the ladder's fresh fights, mostly on bosses and elites with training cards and only level on unseen cards.
